FASHIONS OF TO-DAY,

The holiday season is at its height at pre- sent, and all those who can possibly afford it are out of town revelling in the pleasures of seaside or country resorts. Serges and light woollen materials are in demand for travelling gowns, while they come in very usefully on the many dull and even cold afternoons which an English summer is never without. These toilettes are of the loose bolero and pleated skirt description for the most part, for this fashion has fulfilled the continued yore foretold for it by the chron-

Iciers of fashion, and is not only worn now but will continue to be seen on into early winter in, of course, heavier materials. Many of these costumes are trimmed with braid- ings of all kinds, which become more popu- lar each month, and are certainly much more beautiful than the heavy cloth braids and their jet and gimp fringes which were fashionable in our mothers' youth, and which they are supposed to closely imitate. I wonder with what astonishment our grand- mothers would view the fascinating varieties of silk, chenille, velvet, and tinsel cordings and braidings which are to-day to be pur- chased for the same or even lesser prices than were paid by them for coarse and inartistic work:

The simple toilet depicted has at the first glance nothing to distinguish it from other morning costumes, but it is in the details thereof that the experienced eye detects its ultra-smartness. The short skirt is of the latest cut and fits the figure closely to the knees where inserted pleats give it greater fulness: Here let me impress upon my readers that it is only a slight figure which can wear successfully a clinging frock which perforce reveals the true outlines of the figure. The slightness, however, must not approach too closely to thinness, as any .angularity of outline requires as skilful eon-

A PRETTY 13LOUSE,

cealment as does the "too solid flesh" so often written about, and so serious a worry to the possessor thereof. To return, however, to the costume of our illustratiop ; the skirt, which is of thin navy blue material with a dressed surface, warranted not to spot or shrink, clears the ground all round, while the blouse which is of viyella is spotted with red, a mode mach in favour with the paris- iennes. Viyella is a truly admirable material for blouses, for it wears well and washes as long as there is any of it left to wash, while being partly woollen one can be sure of avoiding any chills when wearing it. A neat lace and muslin embroidered collar and a dull red silk cravat fastened by a buckle finishes the throat.

Blouses are more popular if possible than ever, and are of course a necessity for wear, with a pleated or loose bolero and skirt. The _ .

blouse illustrated is of pale green silk, with a yoke and collar of lace, which finishes at the lower edge in a series of vandykes or points: A double line of insertion runs round the loose pleated part of the bodice, and the sleeves, which are full at the wrists, are also finished by two rows of lace insertion. This blouse could also be expressed in tussore silk, which material continues to be well

A TRAVELLING HAT:

worn in the form of complete toilettes, blouses, or dust cloaks. Shantung silk also is very popular, and in navy blue makes the most delightful dresses, this being a material which lends itself readily •to kilt- ing or box-pleating. . Another valuable property of Shantung silk is that being light it can be packed easily without creas- ing, and emerges quite fresh-looking after a temporary seclusion in one's travelling trunk. For the bodices this silk is very often made into a Norfolk jacket, or into a modified and generally-glorified sailor blouse. , The popularity of the blouse itself, of whatever material it may be, is responsible for the many dainty neck arrangements worn this season, which range from the stock collar and tie to the dainty bow of lace tulle or chiffon. The lightest summer dresses are generally finished by a fluffy bow of some ethereal material tied either at the back of the neck or under the chin. In the former instance it hides a thin neck, while in the atter it softens the complexion, which it also Shows off to advantage. The smart hat depicted is of such a simple character as to render it well suited for wear when travelling by sea or land. It is made of white cloth twisted in shape and trimmed with a bird whose plumage is of the magpie order. The result is a piece of headgear which sits firmly on the head, without that tendency to blow away, which is often the most annoying.. feature of some of the smartest straw hats. It is also warm, yet light, and can be readily worn with a veil whose ends cross at the back, and are brought. round to the side, where they are tied in a coquettish knot. This is a mode much approved of by Americans when globe trotting. Hats generally continue very flat as to crown, and wide as to brim: There is, however, not much in the way of novelty tc Chronicle in the millinery worlds

WOMEN'S CHAT.

Royalty in the Highlands.—The King will be present at the Braemar gathering which will take place at Clunie near Inverca.uld, the middle of September. This is a fortnight later than usual, but the meeting has been postponed to suit the convenience of his Majesty who is not expected at Balmoral before September 12th. , There will then bea large gathering of the Royal Family in the Highlands. The Princev,of Wales who has been recruiting her health in Switzkland will rejoin the Prince and her family, and there will be the Duke and Duchess of Fife, and other near relatives of the Sovereign. The little Princes of Wales, and their sister, who are at Abergeldie Castle thoroughly enjoy ; their life in Scotland, and the two elder onesare already ; learning to handle the fishing rod. ; Royal Residences.—Abergeldie Castle, Which is near ; to Balmoral, was the Highland residence *of the King and Queen after their marriage. Birkhall was also purchased by Queen Victoria, but the King only stayed there once before his marriage, and Queen Alexandra has never'resided there. It is a comparatively small ; place, and at present occupied by Sir Dighton Probyn. The Queen whilst staying at Balmoral has taken many drives, sometitnes accompanied by the-children of the ;Prince and Princess of Wales, and her Majesty greatly ; appreciates the "quiet, refreshing life" on Deeside after la season which has been a very busy and tiring one for their Majesties the King and Queen. I Hatfield House.—Few places are so rich in historical associations as Hatfield, the home of the distinguished statesman whose loss in so universally mourned. It was there, when the place belonged to the Crown, that Elizabeth stayed in semi-imprisonment during the reign of Queen Mary. There is a tree in the park which is pointed out as the one under which Elizabeth was sitting when she heard of the death of her sister, and the hat which she threw off at the time, is one of the most treasured possessions at Hatfield House. It was in the succeeding reign of James I. that the property passed into the possession of the Salisbury family,when the present mansionwas built. In 1835 it waspartially burnt down, but the damage was restore d in exact imitation of the old style of building. _ . . .

Lord Salisbury's -dislike of -" snap-shotters."— Many distinguished people no longer trouble to avoid the' snapshotter." So many devices have been intro- duced into the camera, with the object of concealing its purpose, that it is often quite impossible to tell whether one is being " snapshotted " or not. Conse- quently most people who are the object of attention have given up as hopeless all efforts to avoid the " demon " with the camera. But the late Lord Salis- bury never ceased to be annoyed at being " snap- shotted," and he particularly disliked this when taking exercise on his tricycle. One occasion especially gave him grert annoyance when whilst staying at Contrex6- vine he was snapshotted in the act of wheeling the late Marchioness of Salisbury in a bath chair. A Parisian Romance.—The marriage of M. Pelletan, the French Minister of Marine has provided a romantic story which has greatly delighted the French people. In his impecunious days, when M. Pelletan, was a strugglingjournalist, he received frequent visits from a certain bailiff who however showed him how he could best get over his difficulties. In course of time a firna friendship sprang up between the two men, and in his position as a minister, M. Pelletan interested himself in the matter when the bailiff's sister,Mdlle Denise,who was a schoolmistress in the country wished to be trans- ferred to Paris. Mdlle Denise came,and is now Madame Pelletan, the wife of a man who may possibly one day become President of the French Republic.

Singular Occupations for Women.—The Parliamentary return as to the ages at which people marry, is not so curious as the one relating to the singular occupations in which some women are employed. One woman is returned as a coppersmith, another as a dock labourer, whilst there are some hundreds of women classed under the headings of lightsrmen, bargemen, seamen, boatmen, and pilots. There are women also who are returned as bricklayers, boilermakers, locksmiths plumbers, stablemen, and in fact the whole field or human energy would seem to have its women workers some of whom must follow their daily occupations amidst very singular surroundings. Railway Libraries.—Most people when travelling go armed with literary' matter for railway reading, but it has been suggested that the railway companies might very well provide libraries of magazines and booki in the trains. Most long-distance travelling is now done in corridor trains, in which it would be quite possible to keep a small library of light reading for the use of the passengers. It would not be good for the railway bookstalls, but it would to some extent save passengers the trouble and expense of providing their own litera- ture wherewith to pass the time. Seaside Dangers.—To many holiday makers, a pebbly beach presents an irresistable incentive to throw stones and when plcnicing parties use empty jars and bottles as "cock shies," the i result s very dangerous for children who play bare- footed by the sea. When it is all said, the danger is not so great, but not even sandals will protect the feet from half broken bottles or glass lying about on a loose peb- bly shore. At many seaside resorts men are specially en- gaged to remove refuse from the beach, but it is not al- ways possible to see broken glass, and it is to be feared that the notices posted pp warning people against leaving bottles about, are often disregarded by careless and malevolently disposed trippers. The Rainproof Coat.—The wet summer has been disastrous for many trades, but the makers of rainproo f coats and cloaks must have been, on the other hand, I exceptionally bury. These most necessary garments , have been in almost constant requisition, and one can I well believe that the demand for them has been unpre- cendented.Many now being offered in the shops are smart and inexpensive. The one most favoured by American ' women, and which is very popular, is the Guard's coat, I a somewhat military-looking garment, usually of blue cloth ornamented with big brass buttons. _

The Costliness of Furs:---All furs, we are told, will be very much more expensive this winter. For this, no doubt, the motor rage amongst fashionable people is partly responsible, but for a long time the supply of furs has not been equal to the demand with the result that the best kinds have now become absolutely pro- hibitive in price to anyone who is not a millionaire. Sables will be as fashionable as ever this winter, and almost any price may be given for the best Russian sable. Canadian sable is not so expensive, and this fur is largely used for the ties and neck stoles -which will be generally worn when the cold weather comes. Stoles are tp be very long :Ind very broad in the collar, this season.

MADGE.

TOO MUCH OF A GOOD THING.

I A good illustration,of the evil of haying too much of I a good thing is provided by the experience of a Birming- . ham man, who recently wrote to the bile bean co. a splendid testimonial, havin,, been restored to health by bile beans. He is particulany.fond of pastry and sweet- meats, and during a recent holiday, staying at a board- ing-house at which some capital sweetmeats were put on the table every day, be indulged. ! too freely. The result was an attack of biliousness, and nausea, and a splitting headache. For several days he was very ill, and had it not been for bile beans, probably the whcla of his holiday would • have been spoiled. A similar case was that of Miss M. A. Smith, of East End, Bluntisham, Hunts, although not from the same cause. Her liver became so dis- ordered, that she could not take food without bilious- ness and sickness following. She is a cook by profession, and her illness at times • made her work almost impossible. As long as four days at a time Miss Smith went without food, because the biliousness made the consumption of food an impossibility to her. Many remedies were tried in vain, until she started with bile beans, and then she soon began to recover.. Bile beans at last cured her effectually and permanently. In the course of explaining these facts to a reporter of the "Hunts County News," Miss Smith said ft was over a year ago, at a time I was living at Nottingham, that Iwas advised to try bile beans. I confess I had little faith in them when -I commenced, as I had tried so Many medicines in Vain,but when I feund my appetite returning, my sleep more refreshing,and the retchinf, pains, and biliousness dying away, I could not deny that bile beans were doing more for me than any other medicinell had tried, so I went on with them. In a few weeks they put an end to my biliousness, once and for all. I've had no return of the complaint, and am in better health POW than I've been for years."

Thousands of people are. to-day suffering from liver complaint and biliousness, caused by an excess of sugar or of fat from their food. Bile beans are the surest means of relief in such cases, as the above instances go to show. All chemists now stock Chas. Forde's bile beans, but you are warned .against worthless and harm- ful substitutes.

ANIMALS AT THEIR TOILET.

Animals, says Mr. Henri Coupin, the well-known French naturalist, were certainly the first to use soap, sponges and tooth-brushes. "From time immemorial," he continues, " animals have cleansed themselves, using their tongues as brushes, their saliva as soap, their tails as towels and dusters, and their claws as combs. More- over, many of them take a cold bath whenever they have an opportunity ; indeed, the apes go so far as to boy- cott those among them who do not take proper care of their bodies. Another singular fact about the apes is that those among them who have handsome beards never plunge them 'recklessly into a river or pond, but deli- cately sprinkle bhem with water. Buffon had a chimpanzee who always rinsed his mouth after drinking, and I have heard of a female orang-outang who was an adept in the use of a toothpick." A fly, after it is decapitated, generally spends some seconds in brushing its neck and shoulders with its legs, but even the most zealous advo- cates of cleanliness will hardly claim that its object in doing so is that it may present a suitable post-mortem appearance.

Mr. Roberts rendered the solos "Heavenly Rest" and "Sun of my soul;" being accompanied on the piano by Mr. Wadsworth. Mr. Pearce Williams gave an address on "Christianity and Politics."

CHAPTER XVlll.—Contmued.

"Lunch is waiting for you, sir," she said, "I hope you'll excuse the cooking, sin We have all been rather upset." With these words she left the room, and Walroyd started to go upstairs. Then he suddenly changed his mind, and, going into the dining-room, rang the bell for lunch. He was an eminently practical man, and he did not know what might be awaiting him in the Red Room. It was possible that he might not care for any lunch after he had seen it.

Afterwards he lit a cigar and went upstairs. Walroyd had lived many years in a part of the world where human life is held cheap, and it did not occur to. him that i_aiyould be more reverent not to smoke in the presence of the dead.

The room was in semi darkness as he entered it. The red blinds were pulled down, and the lurid light that Came through them only served to intensify the general gloomi- ness of the apartment. Walroyd could see something lying on the bed, covered with a sheet. He walked over to the windows and drew up the blinds. The sunlight streamed into the room.

Then he went back to the bed and drew down the sheet from the face. The eyes had been closed, but even through the white lids he seemed to see the expression of horror which would have been in keeping with the rest of the dead man's countenance. Walrovd touched the forehead. It was quite cold. Then he carefully examined the head and shoulders. There were five faint blue marks on the white throat, and a small piece of flesh had been torn away. He covered up the face again, drew down • the blinds, and went downstairs. Martin was waiting for him in the porch. He went , into his study, examined the revolver in his pocket—he never went out in those days o 4 terror without this serviceable weapon—took half a dozen more cartridges from a box, and • joined the groom outside. The latter carried a double barrelled gun. The two men crossed the lawn, and passed through a -small wicket gate into the woods. The sunlight filtered through the green arch of leaves overhead, and it was perfectly light. They could see clearly in every direction through the thin stems of the oaks. At first Walroyd looked carefully on the ground and among the undergrowth for some signs of anyone having passed through the wood. Then he remembered that half a dozen men had already been before him, and that any clue of this sort would be utterly useless. So he confined his attention to the distant view through the trees, in ithe hope that something might move across his line of vision. He made Martin walk in front. He was not quite sure of his groom's familiarity with a gun, and he did not want a charge of No. 5 shot in his back.

The wood' appeared to be entirely deserted and they saw nothing but a few rabbits and squirrels. At last; however, they came to the rope web which Tredegar had seen on the night he had followed Walroyd to the rocks. It had evidently been discovered by OP people who had already searched the wood, for all the ropes had been cut, and it lay in a tangled heap on the. ground. Walroyd picked it up and examined it carefully, but he could make nothing of it, and it did not appear to him to have any connection with the man they were in search of. Indeed in its present state it would hardly have con- veyed anything to Tredegar's own mind, if he had not seen it before, for the knotted ,mass of ropes , scarcely bore any resemblance td' a spider's' web. Then they came to the foot of the mound, and Walroyd stopped..

"Do you think he's down that old shaft, sir," the groom whispered. "It would be a tidy hiding place." I should doubt it, Martin," Walroyd answered. "The shaft would be a •hard place to get in and out of. But I will go up and have a look at it. You sit down here and ke4 careful watch." For reasons of his own . John 4Walroy-,d did not want his groom to inspect the shaft too carefully. He climbed up the dreary looking mound of grey rock and shale, and carefully examined the railings round the edge of the shaft. He noticed that in one place the wooden bars had been broken. He looked at the edges of one of the pieces. The fracture was recent, and the wood was still bright and yellow. Then he stepped over the frail barrier and inspected the mouth of the shaft.

A yard from the edge there was a small pile of stones. They were arranged so as to appear a natural heap of debris. He lifted one or two of them, and the end of a steel bar appeared. It was driven deep into the ground. ffe showed no surprise, for the very good reason that he had placed it there him- self. A few yards away a long coil of i-ope lay concealed under a large boulder. He would have liked to have had a look at that as well, and see if it had been discovered or disturbed, but it was impossible with Martin keeping watch below. The top of the mound was partly hidden from the groom's sight, and he could examine the bar without fear of detec- tion. It showed signs of recent use. The steel was still bright and polished. Then he fell down on his knees and peered into the dark pit beneath him. The rugged sides, shored up here and there with balks of timber, and glistening with moisture that had oozed out of the earth, disappeared into absolute blackness. He listened attentively for any sound that might possibly come out from its depths, but the silence 'was only broken by the drip, drip of the water that trickled down its sides. Then he noticed' that the share on one side of the shaft had been furrowed and disturbed, and that several of the pieces of slate were marked with light grey lines as though they had 13-.2en recently scratched with a knife. He took up one or two, and examining, them, threw them impatiently down the shaft. They gave him no clue. ,It was even possible that one of the search ,party had been to the fxlge, and left the marks of his boot on the soft slate.

He descended the slopes and rejoined thti groom. The lqtter had neither seen or heard anything during his master's absence. Then they continued their search through the wood.

Before they., had gone' a hundred yards, however, Martin, whO was ~walking in front. stopped and picked up something from the ground. It was a piece of thin • gold chaip., about nine inches in length. One of the links was broken.

It seemed to him that he had seen it before, or that he had at any rate come across one of a similar pattern. But he racked his brain in vain to remember to whom it had belonged.-- Then the idea struck him that it had probably belonged to Mayanwy Morgan, and had bean torn off in her struggles. There Walroyd learnt from the butler that no trace had been found of the missing girl, nor .had they come across any signs of the murderer of Mr. Morgan.

After dinner Walroyd lit a cigar, and strolled ddwn the drive. He had decided to go to the Cantrips' house and ask Cynthia about the bracelet. He disliked motors, and an accident was pure pleasure to his soul. He stepped forward into the light of the Bleriot lamps. He saw nothing of the beauty of the night, the steely surface of the sea crossed with a long bar of moonlight, the faint stars in the dark blue vault overhead, the long sweep of black cliffs, the white fringe on the water's edge, the great solitude where the noblest part of man can stand face to face with the eternal—none of these things he saw; if he had seen them they would have had no meaning for him. He only looked into the seething fires of his own soul, and the very heavens seemed lurid with the flames.

He waited for ten minutes, but there was no sign of Riley's motor coming down the road. In the far distance, at the end of the street, Walroyd could see the white light of the lamps, but they seemed to be motionless. He stamped his foot impatiently, and then decided to go into the Cantrips' house for a few minutes. He was longing to see Cynthia. It seemed to him that probably he would not be able to have a quiet • conversation with Riley for at least an hour. The man would want some food, and people are always more amenable after they have fed. Riley, too, was a man who rarely went to bed before two o'clock in the morning, and there would be ample time that night to say all that was necessary. Yes, he would certainly see Cynthia. The mere desire to look upon her was stronger than any wish to find out the truth about the bracelet. CHAPTER XX. I'HE SILENCE OF DENNIS RILEY.

THE next morning Riley appeared at the Plas Tredegar in time for lunch, and the tnecanicten took the motor . round to . the stables to overhaul it for the afternoon's run. Riley was proud of his machine, and wished it to look its best and run its smoothest when he was Showing off its paces' before Walroyd.

During lunch the two men talked over old times, and Walroyd told, on an average, about one lie in every minute. His long and cleverly-constructed story of successful speculation made his guest's mouth water, and Riley began _to regard him with rever- ence as one who had conic out "right on top." To an unpractised eye it did not appear a desirable- conveyance. He took his seat by Riley. They decided to leave the mecanicien at home. It was possible they plight like to talk about things which it was not desir- able for a third party to hear. Riley •pulled over one of the• levers, and the throbbing died away as the ear glided down the drive.

" How far can we go without stopping ?" Walrovd asked. •

" Filled her tank up yesterday," Riley answered, "at Llanflhangel. She'll run near two hundred miles. Take you up to London if you like." .

" I shall be satisfied if we get home all right," Walroyd said, drily. But before they had gone three miles, his views on motors were considerably modified. Like most people who have a Jooted objection to this form of locomotion he.. had never been in one in his life. He had alwayil regarded them from the point of view of a man who has to get out of their way; and quiet his restive horses as they pass. To him they repre- sented- a certain amount of smell and dust, and a large amount of danger to all other traffic. But' now as they swept along the smooth road, with less vibration than might be felt in a railway train, he saw that there was another side to .the question.. The sense of speed and power appealed to him. He was a man who liked to do things quickly, and to carry them through with a strong hand. This motor was the mechanical embodiment of his own nature. Woe betide anything that stood in its path. Yet he did not realise that in one essential thing it had the aUvantage of him.. Its brakes. were powerful enough to stop it in its headlong career. Before they had gone ten miles, he had become an enthusiast. Riley; who was a first class driver, and knew his machine from • the :smallest nut to the largest piece of the ' framework, gloried in exhibiting' every phase of its powers. Now he would send it spin- ning along the level road at its top speed, now he would run it up an incline of one in seven at twelve miles an hour ; now he would take it down a low, slope, , till the ,air sang past them, and Warroyd felt as though he would be blown off his seat: Now he would bring it to a dead stop, or turn • it round in the • centre of the road, or take a sharp corner with an inch to spare. He displayed every trick and gambol that.. was in it, and he showed the perfect control he had of its enormous powers, till to Walroyd it seemed almost like a living thing of intelligence • and fi judgment. He explained the. use of the levers and the steering gear and then allowed Walroyd to take his place and steer at the lowest speed along a straight open piece of road. Then he

let him go on the second and third speeds, keeping one hand in .readiness to come to his rescue. But Walroyd's nerve did not fail him. and after five minutes he 'guided the machine with as much composure and confidence as though he had driven one for years. (L; be continued.)

ISEURYi

Universal regret has been expressed at the death of the Marquis of Salisbury. whose ill- ness had been followed with keen anxiety thro • ,ughout the Empire. At 9.15 on Saturday —ll%nt. his lordship passed away peacefully in Is bedchamber at Hatfield, surrounded by all hiS Children save Lord Edward Cecil, who was obliged to return to his military duties in Egypt. The third Marquess of Salisbury, thrice Prime Minister of Queen Victoria, was descended in direct line from that other Cecil who was the most famous of the Ministers of

SALISBUR

Queen Elizabeth. His descent from Lord Burleigh is traced through Robert Cecil, the great Minister's second son, whose career -was hardly less distinguished than that of his father. It was Robert Cecil who received from King James the family titles of Cranborne and Salisbury, and who acquired from the same Sovereign the great house t Hatfield, which he subsequently re- built i its present form, A succession of Earls 01 Salisbury followed through the seven- teenth and eighteenth centuries, playing parts

LORU SALRGBURY'S LIFE AND WORKi

:1 4 11111 1 dultil 111111 111, 1 fur wt. 111 111111

of varying importance in the history .of their country. The seventh earl was in 1789 raised to the dignity of a marquess. The second marquess was a member of one of Lord Derby's Cabinet. His eldest son died unmarried at the age of 44, and the fami!y titles thus passed to the second son, Lord Robert Cecil. Born at Hatfield in 1830, Lord Robert Cecil passed through Eton on to Christchurch: ?Neither at school nor at college was he spe- cially distinguished, hut he seems to have taken an active part in the debates of the Oxford Union Society, and thus prepared himself for a political career; perhaps more effectively than by a profOund study of classics. After leaving Oxford he travelled for a considerable time to complete his education, getting as far as New Zealand. Almost imme- diately after his return to England he became a candidate., in the Conservative interest, for, the vacant seat of Stamford, the home of the elder branch of the Cecil family. He was returned without opposition, and in the ensu- ing session of 1854 took his seat in the House of Commons. In 1866 the actual, though not yet the nominal, leadership of the Liberal party passed, by the death of Lord Palmerston, to Mr. Gladstone, and the question of Parlia- mentary reform was brought forward with a new energy. The bill introduced by Mr. Gladstone dealt,, however, only with the fran- chise, and the Conservatives insisted that such a measure would increase existing anomalies. In the course of the debates much was said' about the working-class voter and his future power. On this text Lord Cranborne delivered' a brilliant speech in a debate. that lasted eight nights, and so shook the position of the Government that a few weeks later they were detained in Committee on the Bill and, a,clause ot small importance. The consequent resigna- tion of the Liberal Ministry involved the return, of Lord Derby to power. Among the politi- cians whom he invited to join his Cabinet' was Viscount Cranborne, who, was offered, and accepted, the important office of Secretary of State for India. Thus, at the early, age of, 36, and without previous, official. experience, Viscount Cranborne stepped into the Cabinet. But he did not fill this capacity long: Discon- tented with the provisions of the Reform Bill concocted by the new Conservative Government Lord Carnarvon, Lord Cranborne and. General Peel resigned. In the spring of 1868 Lord Cran- borne was the strongest opponent of Mr. Gladstone, who moved for disestablishment and disendowment of the Irish Church. On,

HATFIELD HOUSE.

April 12th of this year he became Marquis of Salisbury, .and when he entered the House of Lords his first work was still to defend the Church. Then he dealt With the disestablish- ment question with his usual vigour, saying that the disestablishment of the Irish Church would not have the anticipated effect of abol- ishing Fenianism. In 1869 he was elected :‘-iancellor of the University of Oxford. In the following year Lord Salisbury, the recog- nised leading Conservative in the House of Lords, ssupported the Peace Preservation Bill in a somewhat sarcastic speech, and in the s.ame year the Irish Land Act passed. Then Lord Salisbury distinguished himself as a student of 'foreign allairs in an important speech made at the time of the Franco-Ger- man war. Then he took a very active part in e;:pt)unding the errors of the existing Govein- inent, which was gradually preparing for its and also the rejection of the Irish Uni- Bill. In January, 1874, Parliament

veist

WaS dissolved, and the Conservatives came in with a great majority, and Lord Salisbury became Secretary of State for India a second time, and in this post he gave proof of great administrative ability. Then the Eastern gliestion was reopened; Servia; 'and Monte- negro were at war with Turkey, and Bulgarian

; -srocities came to light. The result was- that .= Conference of the European Powers was held “t Constantinople, Lord Salisbury being, sent envoy from Great Eritain. While here he ,L.(l not obey his instructions or heed the re- monstrances sent out to him from the Foreign Office. The result was that he came into col- lision with the Foreign Secretary, Lord Derby. When Lord Derby's secession followed Lord Carnarvon's, Lord Salisbury was appointed Foreign Secretary. At the same time England was committed to an anti-Russian policy, from the effects of which both countries have ,seri- ously suffered, and are still suffering. The method pursued by Lord Salisbury in opposing Russia was identical with the method which he had already tried to apply to the coercion of Turkey, and which he has since applied to that very purpose with eminent success. He appealed to the Concert of Europe.. In a masterly despatch addressed to the Powers he urged that the Treaty of San Stefano, which Lad just been negotiated between Russia and Turkey removed from the cognisance of the Powers artisles which are modifications of ex

isting treaty engagements and inconsistent with them." The Berlin Congress followed, but as preliminary to that Congress two secret conventions were negotiated byLord Salis- bury, one with Russia and the other with Tur- key. On the death of Lord Beaconsfield, Lord Salisbury succeeded to the leadership of the ,Conservative party. The Examiner, Saturday, August 29, 1903.

WARRINGTON EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

A SHORT MEETING.

Secondary School Arrangements.

There was very little discussion at the monthly meet- ing of the Warrington Education Committee held at the Town Hall on Monday evening, and the whole of the business was transacted in a quarter of an hour. Mr. F. Monks presided and there were also present :—The Revs. John Yonge and Father Wilson, Aldermen Monks and Bolton, Councillors Pierpoint, Broukh, Ellison, Upson, Wilkinson, James Evans and Brophy, Colonel J, D. Buckton, and Messrs. A. J. Jolley, E. J. Guest, G. Walker, J. Bibby, J. Fulton Smith, R. T. Fairclough,E. Plinston, and J. Moor 6 Murray (director of education.) WARRINGTON AND LORD SALISBURY'S DEATH. TOWN HALL FLAG NOT HOISTED.

The Rev. J. YONGE said he should like to suggest in an informal way to the Corporation authorities that on the death of an Englishman like Lord Salisbury the flag on the Town Hall should have been hoisted at half mast. The CHAIRMAN said he was going to make an informal remark. Considering there were so many. Tories in the town one would have thought- Councillor PIERPOINT : It isan oversight. The Rev. J. YONGE : I think it is agreat oversight, and I am. not a Tory as everyone knows. The CHAIRMAN: I will see that the suggestion is conveyed to the proper authority. The flag,l see, is being displayed at the Conservative Club.

From expressions of opinion which had been made by managers of schools, the scheme was a reasonable and equitable one. SITES AND BUILDINGS

PLAYGROUND FOR THE SECONDARY SCHOOL

At a meeting of the Sites and Buildings Committee the Borough Surveyor was instructed to arrange for the carrying out of certain repairs at the School of Art. It was decided that a portion of the land at the back of the Technical institute be levelled and cindered for use as a playground in connection with the secondary school, and that the pathway from the back door of the institute to Museum-street be flagged. The committee decided to request the contractor for the Technical Institute to remove the materials belonging to him from the land at the back of the institute, with the exception of the clerk of the works' office and the hoarding left by him on . the ground, provided that satisfactory arrangements could be made for the purchase of the office and hoarding in question by the com- mittee. The Board of • Education forwarded plans for the alteration of St. Peter's (John-street) School which had beensubmitted to them by its managers, but the committeemade no observations thereon.

Alderman- BOLTON, in proposing the adoption of the minutes, remazked that the repairs to the School of Art were very small. With reference to the land at the back of the Technical School it was proposed to level it and cinder it for use as a playground. The plans for the alteration to St. Peter's School proved satisfactory te'the Board f Education. _ _

WELL-KNOWN FOOTBALLER. Cured of Consumption by Dr.

Williams' Pink .Pi

Mr. Chas. W. Wilson, secretary of the Breaston Foot- ball Club, now residing at 98 Mans-els-street, Nether&ld, told the "Derby Express" that he had been in a Consumption, his weight falling as low as Bst. MTh., and that Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People had saved him. They raised his weight in a very short time to fist. 71b., and no doubt as his strength returned, he put on further flesh.

Derbyshire Football Secretary who was cured of ansaniption, by Dr. Williams' Pink Fills. (Prom a photo by B. Storer, Long-Eaton.)

Mt. Wilson's account of his escape from threatened death, by Consumption is intelligent and interesting. He said

"About sixteen weeks ago I became HI. I did not thinlethe matter of great gravityat first,. but gradually I grew worseand had to seek medical advise. But al- though:skilfully treated I gradually grew worse. I felt very,low ; had scarcely strength to more about, and was quite-unable.to eat or sleep. THE TRAMWAYS. Stockton heath SciTeine Advancing.

: A private meeting of the Stockton Heath Parish 'C'euncil was held in the National Schools on Tuesday, evening for the purpose of discussing plans submitted: by the Warrington Tramways Committee respecting the .proposed light railway to Victoria-square. Mr. J. IL. •Clarke presided, and there were also, present :—Messrs.. Whitley, Bonchier, Pemberton, Mclntyre and Bradshaw (clerk). The Corporation plans showed a double line. 'all along the route. After.- considerable discussion' the. fallowing resolution was passed oa the motion of the Chairman, seconded by Mr.BbuchleAr :—"(1) That the. plan dated June 3rd, 1903; showing the position of the rails be approved, provided that as regards the ' portion of the road opposite the.watl of the Churchyard, if it be found hereafter that in con- sequence of the narrowness of the metalled portion) of the road there is an interference, and inconvenience to traffic, the C trporation Wheil, called upon by. the local authority, fix a cxoss-over to avoid such inconveni- ence. (2) That the position of the posts as shown on the said plan be not approved. Tile Parish Councilwill be pleased to confer with the Warrington Corporation and submit suggestions for improvements. (3), That the clerk write to the.clerk to the County Council draw- ing his attention to the awkward corner of the.road that will be formed at the, shop occupied by Mr. Hatta:key in Victoria-square, when the road is Altered for the War- rington light railway and they express a desire that the County Council wikt take steps to improve, the. road at that point." .

DEATIR OF AN ENORMOUS MAN.

From Chicago.eomes the news of th 4 death of a man only sft. 10in. high, yet weighing 484 lbs. It is said that he was so broad that he could not enter the turn- stiles of the etevated railways in Chicago. The only surface cars in which he could ride, were those having double sliding doors. To make him a complete suit of clothes required 12 yards of cloth, and underwear large enough for Mm was not manrifaotured for him in the United States. Everything he were was therefore made to order in. Europe. His hat was No. 12. The furniture provided fer his use was made with special are, on, account of his great weight.

A RAILWAY MAN'S CASE.

Mr. A. Whitehouse, of Ripon Cottages, Shaw-road, Bushbnry, Wolverhampton, is a railway fireman. For quite a long time he was unable to go to work. He had pains in the lower part of his back so had that he was forced to take to his bed. All the while Mr. White- house was doing everything he, could think of to get well again, but no medicine seemed able to cure him. Gradually he grew worse and finally Nervous Exhaustion added its distress to his butden of pain and illness. He could not sleep at night and grew weaker and winker till he was very low. He says : My work makes it necessary for me. to be called out .at'n moment's notice, and I am exposed to all sorts of weather, and my continued illness was a very serious matter to me.

"I lingered on in this way for a long time till T was persuaded to try Dodds Kidney Pills. The results of the first few doses of this remedy surprised me and I kept on till in a short time T was entirely cured, sound and well, without an ache or pain left. lam as well as ever T was."

The yield of wheat by the acre from Canada Isvastly greater than the yield of the acreage in Australia. A WOMAN'S QUESTION.

There are some questions which affect women mo,;* than men. Perhaps the greatest of all is, how can the housework be done when sickness attacks them? Cur representative had an interview with Mrs. A. Stafford, rg Victoria-road, Dukinfiekt, Manchester. She, haxing suffered for fifteen years from piles, could speak with some authority upon tho matter. ."It is absolutely impossible," said she. "Hove can you work when you are brought to a terribly low, weak state by pain and torture? I found that I eould not even think of doing my housework, and so bad was I that I had not strength even to wash myself. At night my rest would be broken with the pain and agony I had to bear. All this time I tried remedy after remedy in order to get relief for my troubles; but try as I would, no good ever came of my efforts. For a long time I had, given up my case as hopeless, and had prepared to end my 'days in pain and misery; thinking that at 63—my age —it was impossible to ever find a permanent cure. Dr. Morse's Indian Root Pills were, however, brought to my notice. To try one more remedy I thought would not matter. Judge of my surprise, therefore, when with the taking of the first few pills I found my old enemy leaving mo. It was Dr. Morse's Indian Root' Pills that had done it all." THE EXAMINER.

AUGUST 29th 1903.

THE poet of the Seven- THE MUDDLERS. oaks election enjoined the voters in that divi- sion to support" the present muddlers" rather than fly to muddlers that they knew not of. Even this contented person's mind might be changed by the reading of the Report of the Royal Commis- sion on the War in South Africa. It is impossible to believe that any sort of Government, whatever degree of incompetence it might exhibit, could make such a criminal mess of things as was made by the existing administration before and during the South African War. The Report is a docu- ment of a rather mild character. The Commis- sioners have evidently wished to save the face of the Government as much as possible. They have not desired to make the war once more a burning question, and therefore they have trod delicately. But the facts are such as speak for themselves,and they exhibit his MAJESTY'S present advisers in such a predicament as must be painful even to their keenest critic. The Government won the last General Election on the khaki issue. It contrived to create the impression that it was do- ing very well in a difficult situation, and that no- body else could be trusted either to conduct the war or to bring it to a conclusion. For the earlier blunders and calamities it very skilfully cast the blame on others, and particularly on the Intelli- gence Department. Ministers pretended that they had not been informed. Mr. BALFOUR was sur- prised that the Boers were mounted; Sir MATTHEW WHITE RIDLEY was astonished that they possessed guns; Lord SALISBURY wished to be informed how he and his colleagues could possibly be expected to note that munitions of war had been sent over in piano-cases. Either this was pretended ignorance or it was criminal ignorance. The Intelligence Department had not failed in its duty. It had sent in the fullest possible particulars about the Boers and their preparations. Lord WOLSELEY, also, without being two insistent, had dealt faith- fully with the Government as to the force that would be required if the negotiations with the Boers should result in war. But the reports of the Intelligence Department were hot attended to, and for political, which means party, reasons, the money that was declared in August, 1899, to be necessary for the despatch of 10,000 men was not provided. The Intelligence Department reports do not even seem to have been read by the res- ponsible Minister. Lord LANSDDWNE Says they were never officially ',communicated to him, and it is to be assumed that whatever is not officially communicated does not exist. When we were on the very brink of war the Government failed to make good a deficiency of stores which was reported both by the Admiralty and the Army Board. There was no plan of cam- paign; there was no adequate preparation of any kind; there was no belief among Ministers that the country had anything serious in hand. They thought that we were about to frighten President KRUGgR by shaking a few bayonets in his face. The calculation was that the army would be back again at home to eat its Christmas dinner. But these absurd miscalculations were not made through lack of warning. It was in 1897,tw0 years before the outbreak of the war, that Lord WOLSELEY foretold tile peril in which Ladysmith

===== 0002601_19030829_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
would be placed in the event of hostilities. I 0 June, 1899, he estimated the number of troops that would be required in a certain emergenc9.? and he recommended the mobilisation 01 an Army Corps and a cavalry division Oil Salisbury Plain. Two months later he recom- mended the despatch- of 10,000 men. 1311 the Government attended to nothing. It merely loitered and muddled and trusted to luck. It had been accurately informed as to the probable extent of the Boer forces, yet when the war broke out it had only 22,000 troops in South Africa, where it afterwards had to send over 400,000. One hundred millions of pounds sterling, one of the Coma'ssioners thinks, knight have been saved over the war if the Government had spent money a little more freely at first. But the neglect of adequate preparation for a war which Ministers now assure us was inevitable was deliberate. It Ras coincident with the neglect of the safety of the nation. Lord LANSDOWNE had to confess to the Commission that "we were notsufficiently prepared,even for the equipment of the comparatively small force which we had already contemplated might be employed beyond the limits of this country in the initial stages of a campaign." Why were we not prepared for this "comparatively small" task ? There were political reasons. The fact was that the [Government which came in on the cordite vote dared not face Parliament and tell the truth. It preferred to take at haphazard all the risks of whab afterwards turned out to be a war almost as des- tructive of life as that in the Crimea, and wore than twice as costly. It is now clear enough whY Mr. CHAMBERLAIN sprang his new Fiscal policy 01:1 the country. He knew that this report Wes coining out. He knew what it would contain, sod he %oped to sweep it away in the flood of his "raging and tearing campaign." But the Repo will not be swept away. With all its mildness (4 tone, it is a terrible indictment against an incoar potent Government, and a Government that Was worse than incompetent. A nation which would support such an Administration as the present in face of the Rtiport on the war would deserve all the calamities that even such Ministers as LOrd LANSDOWNE are capable of producing.

Mr. Chamberlain's proposals, so far as they have been made public, have received a rude rebuff sw the hands of the sturdy Argyllshire electors. There hs.s been a big change in the complexion of this constituency, no less than eleven hundred votes having been transferred from the Tory to the Liberal side, giving Mr. Ainsworth the smashieg majority of 1586. One does not need to look for the reason. Mr. Chamberlain's preferenti tariff policy was the piece de resistance betweell the two candidates,whose views were as far divide as the poles. The result is positively startling: and must go down badly with the master o' Highbury, who could not have expected his call- paign to open so inauspiciously. The food tax,as " Star " graphically put the case, is being “snovis, under," and if th 3 fall continues at the preseav rate, there is very poor prospect of the tax ever being dug out. Bye-eletions are the pulse of WE:, people, and the violent throb in Argyllshire canoo' fail to make a big impression. There has been o°, change in the personnel of the electorate—alms:A; exactly the same number of people voted—bi-': there ;has obviously been a sweeping change (31' opinion. Liberalism and Free Trade have clearli caught the flowing tide, and it bodes ill for al advocates of Protection at the approaching generw election.

Passive resistance is increasing—shall we say. jIl strength ?—all over the country. It is also corniot nearer home. There have been prosecutions, sales by auction after distraint for non-payment, , the education rate, at places so adjacent as Altrl cham, San ibach and Frodsham. A Oiti2011"„ League, for passive resistance to the Act has bee" formed in Warrington, and also in St. Eleitt and we may reasonably expect some of the rae7,„ hers to figure shortly in the local police courr, That they will not shirk such an ordeal is certsll3o "Passive resistance " may sound rather mild, Ile it is indicative, nevertheless, of an unalterab, determination not to be taxed in order to supn sectarian teaching in the elementary schools. Tb!, powerful weapon, the Nonconformist consciee'' is being gradually but menacingly raised aloft.

The people of Warrington will have an oPP2r, tunity on Saturday next (September sth), vluef a fancy dress parade and carnival will take placeP°l contributing towards the funds of the 110,60 National Lifeboat Institution. It is six Ye3`b since a similar appeal was made, and althoog.. there have been many appeals in the meantime f' re war funds and local charities, it may, we think, II safely assumed that the Warrington public vg. id respond handsomely to the appeal which is beg* made. Trade in Warrington has been far frt good this year, and the pockets of the people 5 not as well lined as could be wished still there is reason to hope that the Wm-- of the Lifeboat Institution will not allowed to materially suffer. The institution solely on the voluntary contributions of all class—f throughout the country for the maintenance its great life-saving fleet. When it is remember% that there are 288 of the institution's lifeboat? °L; the shores of the United Kingdom and that elro its establishment in 1824 no fewer than lives have been saved, some idea of the exteotZo this great rescue work "for those in peril on sea" will be obtained. The whole of the moi3e,fs raised by Lifeboat Saturday demonstrations e, devoted entirely to the payment of lifeboatle and grants to their widows and children. 110 local committee is doing its best to make 0? Saturday's demonstration a success, and havi7o done their duty well, it will be for the onlooke to do likewise.

Warfare has broken out again, and the London •and North-Western Railway Company have made 'a side attack on Arpley cannons, which the Cor- poration are manfully resisting. The Town Clerk ••reports that the Railway Company have inserted lin the agreement relating to Arpley-road a condi- tion "that when called upon the Corporation shall reroove the guns opposite Arpley Station." This condition is naturally objected to, and we are sure that the objection will be supported by everybody in Warrington. Arpley, without the cannons, would be like " Hamlet" minus the Prince. They have stood in their present suitable position for nearly half a .century—since 1858, we believe,—and are not only Objects of interest, but, a distinct ornament to a station noticeable for nothing more than its gloom • and ugliness. Why the Railway Company should desire to get rid of these relics of Sebastopol, we "nnot imagine, unless they have some big exten- 'lions in view. A considerable increase in station accommodation and conveniences might possibly be looked upon—even in Warrington—as a reason- able excuse for moving the cannons, but unless the ca.use is adequate, the objection to any interference with Arpley cannons will most certainly be of a 'determined character.

The air has been full of complaints about the unsettled weather for some time past, but the members of our local police force have special cause to feel aggrieved. Continuous rain caused them to postpone their annual sports a week ago. but to their thagrin the conditions on the second -day selected were almost as bad. There wtts not much inducement to spectators to gather in strong force and the hopes of the promoters for a record attendance were dashed to the ground. The most regrettable feature is that the funds of deserving institutions will suffer, but the policemen, who Worked indefatigably to arrange a successful meeting, will have the consolation of knowing that they are in no way to blame. We can only wish them better luck nest year.

The Warrington Board of Guardians is engaged in the solution of he accommodation difficulty at Fie Workhouse. The returns show that the num- ,°;3r.of Persons seeking refuge in "the evening of mum days"under the roof of this vast hall of Poverty is steadily increasing. This is perhaps _ 3' a natural result of the increased population of the district, and is not an alarming feature in itself. It is a difficulty, however, which has to be faced, and the Guardians have shown no disposi- tlon to shirk their duty. A committee has carefully considered the matter, and it will be some relief to sorely-oppressed ratepayers to learn that an easy and inexpensive means 11" been devised to meet the difficulty. When the hospital patients were removed to the new Union Infirmary they left behind them an old hospital block of buildings which had done excellent service for a long number of years. It is new proposed to "do up" these premises—to use the phrase of the Guardians—and provide accom- Paodation for 40 additional beds for the ordinary Workhouse inmates. The cost will be i compara- tivel- y small, and the only addition to the staff s the appointment of a "handy-man." This gentle- is not one who will come under the descrip- tion of the "handy man in navy blue," but he is to be provided with a working suit and a duty suit nevertheless, and will be called upon to do odd Jobs about the workhouse such as glazing, painting and plumbing. Tradesmen in this particular line Will hardly relish the idea of such an appointment and expsession was given to their views during the discussion on Wednesday. The appointment •of the" handy-man" was made all the same.

Brighouse, County Coroner, conducted an inquest at the Police Station on ,Tuesday touching the death of William John Cadlip,s2, years of age,a crane driver, who resided at 230, Winwick-road. —The evidence showed that last Friday night deceased was retiring to bed when he fell downstairs. He did not appear Much worse for the accident and slept on the sofa, in the kitchen during the night. The following morning his wife came downstairs and found him lying unconscious on the floor. Dr. Ferguson attended him and he was afterwards removed to the Infirmary, where he died on Saturday night.. —A verdict of "Accidental death" was returned. H. M. BEST BOOTS FOR ALL.—UNDER the CLOCK.—Advt.

Another Fire Alarm.

There was another alarm of fire on Monday after- noon but fortunately the flames were extinguished with little difficulty. It was about half-past four when information reached the Fire Engine Station of an outbreak at the British Workman refreshment rooms. A detachment_ of men under the direction of Superintendent Turner was quickly on the scene with a steam fire engine. It was at once seen that the engine would not be required and with the aid of a hose pipe the fire was soon put out. It is stated that a beam inside a flue became ignited and but for the prompt measures taken to deal with it the fire might have been a serious one.

UNDER the CLOCK or GOOD BOOTS.—Advt, Shop Gutted In Fennel-street.. - The lock-up shop of \lr. 11. AkKeon, grocer and provision dealer, 30, Fennel-street. was practically. destroyed by fire on Sunday morning. Shortly after 4 o'clock P.C. Dyson discovered the outbreak andigave the alarm. The fire brigade, under the command of Superintendent and Deputy-Superintendent Turner, quickly turned out with the engine "Major," but upon their arrival the flames had obtained a firm hold. Efforts were at once made to prevent the fire from spreading to adjoining property, and in this the fire- men were succoisful. The shop and several other rooms were completely gutted, but the damage, which is estimated at £3OO, is covered by linsurance. flow the fire originated is a mystery.

Juvenile Rechabites' Picnic. • The members of the Jonaelab Juvenile Tent (which is held at Brick-street Mission, Brick-street) along with the Superintendent, B. Warburton, J. Shaw,. J. Thorniley, W. Bradshaw and Secretary (T. Bibby) journeyed to Frodsham on Saturday last by the 12.30 train from Bank Quay Station.. The members turned up in full force and after participating in the various amusements on Mersey View were provided with a good tea out of the Tent funds. The day was all that could be desired, with the exception of a sharp shower just after tea, and all seemed to thoroughly enjoy themselves, getting back to Warrington about 7.30 p.m. thoroughly tired.

LADES EST GLACE BOOTS and SIDES, UNDER the CLOCK, BRIT/ GE-STREET.—AcIut- Independent Order of Good Templars. . .

The quarterly session of the South-West Lancashire District Lodge was held on Saturday at Tuebrook, representatives being present from all parts of the district, Warrington I was :Well represented. The D.C.T. (Rev. J. A. Lee) presided. The Chairman's report dealt with a number of matters. including the fact that a lodge has been recently instituted .in Liberia as the result of the initiation of the . Chief Justice of Liberia, the Hon. Z. B. Roberts, when he was visiting .Liverpool. The reports of the D. Sec. (Bro. Morris Jones) and the D.S.J.T. (Bro. FL Newman) showed considerable activity, several new branches having been opened. Electorial matters were dealt with by the D.E.S. A resolution to the effect ,that the Grand Lodge be petitioned to alter the date of meeting from Easter week until the first week in August was introduced bv the "Temperance Mission" Lodge, Warrington, and after discussion, carried. It was decided to hold the November session at War- rington. In the evening a public meeting was held, presided over by the Rev. J. Rowland Parker. The following resolution was adopted, viz :—" That inasmuch as the Justices of the .Peace have for 350 years been entrusted with the powers of refusing in the public interests to continue or .renew publicans' licences, this meeting declares its nnconpromising opposition to every attempt, direct or indirect, to create a vested interest in liquor licences as being Vicious in principle and injurious to the public welfare which it is the duty of all citizens to safeguard and maintain." Speeches were delivered' by the Rev. J. A. Lee and Messrs. M. Jones, G. 13: Walker, and G. S. Schofield.

PUBLIC BENEFILT, UNDER the CLOCK BEST for BOOTS in LANCASHIRE.—Advt. Warrington and the Late Lord Salisbury.

The fla—gs at the Conservative Clubs have been floating at half mast this week as a mark of respect to the late Lord Salisbury. Many years ago his Lord- ship passed through the Central Station, Warrington, on his way to Liverpool where he had promised to address an important political meeting. The local Conservatives decided to present him with an address and, in the presence of a large number of persons who had assembled on the platform, it was handed to him through the carriage window by the late Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart. Lord Salisbury made no speech, briefly thanking Sir Gilbert for presenting the address.

In the presence of many sorrowing relatives and friends the funeral of John Handford. Corbett (9), of Belmont-avenue, Latchford, who was drowned in the Bridgewater 'Canal, last week, near Black Bear Bridge, and of John Flodgeson Wilson (10), of 6, Lyon-street, who was drowned while attempting to rescue Corbett, took place on Saturday afternoon. An impressive service was held. at the Latchford Wesleyan Chapel, the Dead March in "Saul" being played on the organ by Mr. Ditchfield, after which the funeral cortege, which was headed by a number of day. and Sunday school Scholars, proceeded on its way to Warrington. On arrival at Warrington Bridge the body of Corbett 'was taken on to St. Thomas's Churchyard, Stockton Heath, where it was interred, while the remains Of the little hero, Wilson, were laid to rest in the cemetery. Hundreds of people witnessed the funeral ptocession, and much sympathy was expressed for the bereaved parents. ASK for CUNNENGHAM'S HIGH-CLASs NIINERA.L WATERS.—Advt. An Interesting Roman Tablet.

In view of the many Roman discoveries at Wilders- pool by Mr. T. lay, -it will be of interest to our readers to learn that some months ago a Roman altar, in a good state of preservation, was found by the Tyne Commissioners' workmen in the bed of the river at Newcastle,.near the site of the ancient Roman bridge. Their operations have now brought to light a tablet of grey millstone grit, which is flawless, and on which there is =inscription, of which the follow- ing is a translation :—" To the Emperor Antoainas August Pius and Father of his Country. This work is done by detachments of the Second Legion Auzust,of the Sixth Legion Victorius,and Second Legion Valeria Victrix." The author of the tablet is recorded upon it as Julius Verias of the Augustan Legion and Pro- prcetor." The discovery has occasioned. considerable interest among northern antiquarians

H. M. ASHTON'S Lurg TOMO is the KENO OF ToNtcs.-133, SANKEY-STREET, Warrington.—Advt. Choir Trips.

The animal excursion in connection with the choir pf the Sacred Heart took place on Saturday, Belle Vue !being visited. The party left the Central Station by the 1.55 train, and on arriving at Manchester visited the different places of interest, afterwards taking the tram for Belle Vue. Here tea was served at half past .four, after which sails on the lake and the" Ocean wave," and an inspection of the gardens, followed bk a dance, enabled the time to pass pleasantly away un- til dusk, when. the fireworks display was witnessed. The choir returned to Warrington at 10.45, thanks being dile to Mr. J. Gibbins for his excallent arrange- ments. The choir of the Legh street Primitive Methodist Chapel held their annual picnic at Chester on Saturday. After walking round the walls of the city the merhbers journeyed by steamboat up the river to Eccleston Ferry, where tea was partaken of. The choir afterwards rendered several partsongs for which they were heartily encored, and publicly thanked by all the andience assembled in the large dining room. A: visit was paid to Eaton Hall, and there the choir sang a glee near the gates. As the last boat was missed, the members were compelled to walk,reaching Chester in time for the, 9.10 train arriving at Warrington about 10: The arrangements were satisfactorily carried out by the choirmaster Mr. Albert Spruce. The Pedestrian Craze. Another walking contest took place on the outskirts of Warrington on Saturday evening.' The competitors were ,: Jones. and W. Clayton, the route being through Stretton, Whitley, Daresbury, and Hatton to Appleton Thorn, each competitor to wheel a barrow. There was a special prize given for the best decorated barrow, the prize being awarded to J. Jones. After the judging of the barrows, which. was kindly under- taken by Miss Britch and Miss Crimes, the competitors faced the starter, Jones beinc, allowed three minutes start of Crayton. Thaiword having been given, Jones started off at a brisk pace, and it was easily seen he meant making the best of the start. Clayton started off smartly, and it was thotight he would overhaul Jones, but the latter stuck to the task, and it was seen at Daresbury that he had the better of his man. Clay- ton contented himself with keeping his rival under the three minutes allowed. Leaving Daresbury rain began to fall heavily, hut each competitor stuck to his task manfully, Jones leading well through Hatton, Stretton, and back to Appleton Thorn. Time: Jones, Ihr. nmin. ; Clayton, lhr. 55.1 min.

Mishap to a Funeral Party. A funeral party had a narrow escape from injury on Thursday afternoon owing to the shying of a horse: Shortly before three &Clock three ladies and a youth were being conveyed in a cab to the cemetery, and they had with them in the conveyance a small coffin containing the body of a child. When turning into Afanchester-road from Morley-street the horse attached to the Cab took fright at a passing tramcar. . The driver did his utmost to hold the horse in cheek, but the wheel of the vehicle , getting in a rut, the cab was overturned. The driver was thrown from the__ box and injured_ about the

chest and face. The occupants of the cab .were assisted out of the vehicle, and escaped with nothing more serious than a severe shaking. It is supposed that the step attached to the cab prevented the cab from going entirely over. The party afterwards pro- ceeded on foot to the Cemetery with the coffin, where the interment took place. The damaged vvhicle was taken back to the stables, and another sent to the Cemetery for the conveyance home of the funeral party. GIVE THEM A TRIAL.—ALF. TAYLOR'S wonder- ful boots for everybody. ..( Over 1,000 Miles With a Barrow.

• An extraordinary feat or pedestrianism has been accomplished by James Robinson, of Ba.ttersbylane, who returned to Warrington on Tuesday evening after walking 1,000, Miles with a barrow in twenty days. As may be imagined he was warmly congratulated by numerous friends who had assembled to witness his arrival. Robinson reached Warrington Bridge about eight o'clock, having just walked 52 miles by way of Rainhill, St. Helens, and Wigan. He has walked on an average 50 miles a day for the past 20 days—with the exception of Sundays,- When he rested—and but for a little soreness of his feet he is still comparatively fit and well. He walked in clogs, which were presented to him prior to commencing his .task, and had a light walking attire. During the course of his long tour he has called at Police Stations and shops along the route and obtained signatures to a paper giving the time he passed through various places, and these have been in the possession of Mr Berry, of the Glassmakers' Arms, which place Robin son made his headquarters. Since leaving Warring- ton Robinson has walked to London, . and back and to Sheffield, and back. He has also • toured in the Blackpool district, and has been tollanchester, Wigan, St. Helens and Liverpool. Robinson follows the occu- pation of a labourer, is 54 years of age, and has a amily of ten.

ONE MILE WALKING HANDICAP (WARRINGTON POLICE

IN UNIFORM). Four competitors—Constables Woods, Whittaker, Nevy, and Tolley—turned out for the one-mile walk. Tolley had 130 yards start from Whittaker, who toed the scratch line, whilst Woods and Newey were each conceded 120 yards. Tolley maintained the lead for several laps, but was eventually overhauled by Woods. The last-named competitor walked in fine style for most of the distance, but towards the close he was seen to "break" several times, and was disqualified although he was the first to breast the tape. The first prize—a six-bottle cruet frame value £2—Went to P.C. Whittaker who walked splendidly from beginning to end. Tolley carried off the second award—a set ofcarvers, value £l. The time was 9mins. 27sec.

The Postponed Meeting.

The members of the Warrington police force have been singularly unfortunate this year in experiencing such depressing weather conditions for their 'annual sports. It was disappointing enough to have to post- pone this popular meeting last week because the incessant rain made it quite impossible to carry the events through with any degree of • success. Con- sequently although the arrangements had been com- pleted and everyone was anticipating a record gate it was reluctantly decided to postpone the sports until last Thursday afternoon. Further disappointment was in store for the promoters. Many anxious glances were directed skywards on Thursday morning and the outlook was distinctly unpromising. Early in the afternoon the rain, never so unwelcome, began_ to descend, and for several hours the downpour continued without the slightest abatement. Athletic enthusiasts, however ardent, could hardly be expected to brave the elements on such a day, and the attendance—although it might have been much worse—suffered very con- siderably. It had been hoped that about 6,000 spec- tators would witness the sports, but as it was between 1,500 and 2,000 put. in an appearance. Towards four o'clock the rain cleared off and later the clouds rolled away and there was welcome sunshine. There was a capital entry for the various events, but naturally the wretched weather had the effect of greatly reducing the number of competitors. who actually turned out. In some heats the course had to be covered merely as a matter of form. Owing to the sodden condition of the track, cyclists had to exercise great precaution and several spills occurred. Fortunately no one was seriously hurt.. Local com- petitors acquitted themselves particularly well in the cycle events, much to the satisfaction of the spectators, and the exciting finish in the two mile handicap was quite a feature of the meeting. It can- not be said that the programme was carried through very expeditiously as there was considerable delay between some of the events, and it was a quarter to seven when the final race had been decided. An ac- ceptable diversion was provided in the shape of a military tournament by a detachment of soldiers from Orford Barracks under the direction of • Sergeant Instructors Stamford and Sweeney. The items were submitted with commendable precision and skill and the men are to be complimented upon their exhibition. The following is a complete list of the officials : President : Mr. L. Talbot, Chief Constable ; Vice- President: Detective Superintendent Monaghan; Judges: Councillor D. Tinnion, Messrs. Harvey and Babbitt; Referee and Judge of walking : Mr. Walter Platt, N.C.A.A. ; Marksmen : Messrs. A. Booth, F. Crabtree, D. Wylie and P.C. Robinson; Stewards': Inspector Taylor and Rowbottom, Sergeants Smith and Williamson, Detective Meacock. and Barlow, P.C.'s Newey, Hayes, Stewart, Edwards, and Johnson: Clerks Of Course : Chief Inspector Phillips and P.C. Dennett ; Starter: Mr. T. Wainwright, Salford Harriers ; Secretary : Sergeant W. Evans ; Assistant Secretary: P.C. R. Hodgkiss. A special meed of Praise is due to Sergeant Evans 'who as secretary worked indefatigably to make the arrangements as complete as possible. The proceeds were in Aid of the Warrington Infirm- ary, District Nursing Association, and Police Athletic Club, and it is a matter for regret that such werthy institutions should suffer in consequence of the bad weather. During the afternoon the Borough Band discoursed an excellent selection of music under the conductorship of Mr. T. Griffiths.

MR. R. A. NAYLOR AND THE - CARNAR VON CONTEST.

THE WORKINGMAN'S "LIFT-UP'.'

At the Conservative Club, Conway, on Tuesday night, a semi-private meeting of Unionists was held to wel- come Mr. R. A. Naylor, the Unionist candidate for Carnarvon Boroughs, and moved a vote of condolence with the family of the late Lord Salisbury. which was seconded by the Hon. FL Lloyd Mostyn and curled. Mr. Naylor, said he came before them to appeal V) the electors in the present crisis in the national history to think not of themselves, not a a party, but of their country. Mr. Chamberlain's scheme would gives, magnificent lift up for the working people, who wanted it at the present time. If Mr. Chamberlain was helped by the country, he would carry out the finest piece of work any statesman could set his hand to ; but if the present opportunity were lost it might never occur again.

The " Political Tramp." Mr. Lloyd George M.P., in a Caustic Mood.

A meeting of the Liberal Associations of the Car- varvon Boroughs was held at Cbrnarvon on Tuesday. Mr. D. Lloyd George, M.P., and Mr. J. H. Whiteley, M.P., were present, and there was a large attendan3e of dele- gates and others. Mr. Lloyd George referring to the political situation said he understood that he was going to be opposed at the nextelection. (Laughter.) The boroughs had re- jected two or three gentlemen, admirable citizens and very highly respected neighbours, not from lack of per- sonal respect, and he did not think the Carnarvon Boroughs would reject him for the first available political tramp—(laughter)---who presented himself at the gates with the only claim for their consideration that some time or other he had occupied a casual ward in some part of the constituency. (Loud laughter and applause.)

The " Naylorian Political Economy."

The "Carnarvon Herald" comments as follows on the candidature of Mr. R. A. Naylor :—" We believe he makes some pretence to being an :iuthority on fiscal matters. But if he is correctly reported by his friends. it is evident he has yet to learn the a b c of Political Economy. If he can give over writing dogged hymns for a few days, he might make an effort to master some elementary•booklet on the science—say, the "Tales" of Harriet Martineau. Hiving mastered this primer,he might then try to nnderstand John Stuart Mill's classic "Prin- ciples,' awl Adam Smith's "Wealth of Nations." As a lighter dish, he might try a stew of what Mr. Chamber- lain slid, about fettering trade, in those cliys before he joined the "gentlemen "—that is to say, the land- owners—" of England." At present, Mr. Naylor is simple-rninied enough to believe that a nation is enriched by sending goods out of the country, and impoverished by goods being brought into the couritry. . . , . . Why do timber merchants, at Warrington and else- where, continue to "ruin the country' by importing deal boards from the Baltic? Will Mr. Naylor under- take to do his share towards stopping this disastrous work? We trow not! There is only one word that cnn adequately describe the Naylorian Political Economy. It is, IFudge."

THE WRITER OF OUR NEXT "SPECIAL"

Mr. John Williams I3enn, who is to write our special article next week on"Munimpal Progre.ss,"is a well-known figure at all gatherings of London Liberals. He had been a Progressive member of tbe London Comity Council since its creation, and has taken an active part in all the great movements which have placed the London County Council in the vanguard of progressive bodies, while the old Corporation of the City of London remains a by-word of Toryism and stagnation. In 1892 Mr. Benn represented St. George's-in-the-East in Parlia- ment, but in 1895 lost his seat to one of the numerous undistinguished Conservatives who won on the cry of Old Ag4.33ensious, &c. . _ _ _ _ _

Britiiin's loss was London's gain, for Mr. Benn could give more time in civic affairs, and iii the same year he became Vice Chairman of the Council.

He is, however, hest known as Chairman of the High- ways Committee of the London County Council,and in that capacity has been instrumental in promoting the present movement all over England in favour of cheap, quick, publicly controlled traction. When it was re- solved a year ago to reconstruct the London Liberal Federation, Mr. Williarn"Benn was unanimously selected as Hon. Secretary, and is devoting himself with his customary energy to'the revival of London Liberalism. Will he succeed? Nous verrons. London certainly needs it.

Death ,of Mr. Peter Weave, of Moore.

• A well-known and much respected inhabitant o f Moore passed away on Tuesday morning in the person of Mr., Peter Gleave, • grocer and provision dealer, who for the past twenty- five years has been sub- postmaster of the . village. The deceased gentleman represented Moore on the Runcorn Board of Guardians and Rural Dis- trict Council, and was present at a meeting of the Guardians- so recently as a fortnight ago. The circumstances\ attending his death were such as, to necessitate an inquest, which was crmductedby Mr. R. Dobson, Coroner, on Wednesday._ —The evidence showed that while building a wall deceased scratched his hand hut thought nothing of it at the time. Subsequently blood-poisoning set in and resulted in his death.--. 1 verdict in accordance with the medical testimony was returned, and the jury passed a vote of condolence with the relatives.— References to the sad event were made at the meeting of Runcorn Board of Guardians on Tuesday, and on the motion of Mr- J. Stokes, seconded by Mr. S. Woodward, it was decided to forward a letter of sympathy to the relatives.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The Education Rate.

A meeting of this Board was held at Dutton Work house on Tuesday, Mr. Joseph Stokes presiding. There were also present :—The Rev. Dr. Gastaldi, Messrs. S. Woodward, T. Wright, G. Pilling, J. Bolshaw, J. Moseley Williams, W. Mounfleld,S. Taylor, T. Wood, J. Knowles, T. Lea, J. Douglas, C. Roylance, T. Clark, J. Darlington, E. Bretherton, G. F. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Hough (assistant clerk), T. Cooke, J. Riley and W. de Prez (relieving officers) and J. Ctmnah (master).

TWO LOSSES BY DEATH.

The Chairman said they we,re met under very sad circumstances which had been intensified since he entered the room. Their hearts had all been moved by the great loss they had sustained in the death of their late respected member, Mr. Bradford, who gave a great part of the best of his days for the public welfare. He was known for many years as a member of the old High- way Board at Daresbury and when the change of the law which merged the Highway Board into the District Council took place he became a member of the latter authority. He was with them for some years, but owing to age and infirmities he gave way to his son. He was a man who was always honoured and respected and they would miss him very much. They hoped that for many years his son might fill the position which had been so worthily occupied by his father. Since he entered the room he had heard of the death of one who was present at the last meeting—Mr. Peter Gleave, of Moore. He took an active interest in the affairs of the District Council and the Board of Guardians and he would be very much missed. He moved that letters of sympathy be sent to the bereaved rolatives. Mr. S. Woodward, chairoan of the District Council, seconded and Mr. Moseley Williams supported the motion which was carried in silence, all the members standing.
