===== 0002601_19030829_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BOA.RDING ouT OF CHILDREN.

Admiral Jones-Parry had given notice to move that 15 acres of land be purchased as a site for the erection .of Cottage Homes. The Admiral was not present and Mr. Woodward said they had an intimation at the last meeting of the District Council that the question would be indefinitely post- poned. Mr. Wood thought it was a pity that Admiral Parry had allowed the question to drop. He was rather pleased to see that the matter was to be brought for- ward, but he thought the Admiral must be afraid at the magnitude of the scheme he proposed. The question of boarding out Workhouse children would be forced upon them before very long. Other Unions were adopting that course and he would give notice that at the nest meeting he would move that a committee be appointed to consider the advisability of boarding out boys and girls. Mr. Woodward said this was an entirely different question from that which Admiral Parry should have introduced. He thought there was a misapprehension in Mr. Wood's mind as to the value of the two systems. They already boarded out children at institutions provided for that purpose, and he did not think there would be au diffi- culty about the matter if they knew where homes could be obtained to accommodate all the children at a reasonable cost. The question of establishing Cottage Homes was a very much larger question than either Admiral Parry or Mr. Wood had better bring forward at present. Mr. Wood said he would never have dreamt of sit p- porting Admiral Parry's proposal. His suggestion was that they should make use of the institutions already provided in various parts of the country. He was fully persuaded that the children could be boarded out at as small a cost as was at present incurred to maintain them. At boarding-out institutions the children would be taught trades and the Board would not lose sight of them in after years like they did 'at present. He was convinced that they had not been doing their duty to the children in the past. All the Catholic children were being boarded out but there were no Protestant children receiving similar favours. The matter then dropped.

DELEGATES To Tim POOR LAW CONFERENCE. On the motion of Mr. Woodward, seconded by Mr. Williams, it was decided thatlMessrs Knowles,Mounfleld and Douglas, together with the master and clerk, should represent the Board at the. North Western Poor Law Conference to be held at Blackpool.

FINANCIAL MATTERS. THE EDUCATION RATE.

Mr. Woodward on behalf of the Finance Committee reported that there was a balance in hand of £948 16s 10d The calls for the ensuing half year would be : General County purposes, £2,3:32 4s 4d; police purposes, £349 15s. 2d.; police district purposes, £766 Is. 7d.; total, £3,448 Is. Id. For the purposes named the amount required wa.s £234 more than for the previous half year and £350 more than the call for the corresponding period last year. In'addition £1,865 145.5 d. would be required for educational purposes, £466 2s. 2d. being. for higher education, and the remainder for general educatien purposes. The education rate would be equal to 2d. in the £. The total amount required was £2,200 More than last year's amount, this being largely accounted for by the new education rate. Mr. Wool said the information would be of interest to the people who intended to resist the education rate. (Laughter). The committee's minutes were approved.

RAILWAY FATALITY NEAR WIDNES,

At the Liverpool City Coroner's Court, on Tuesday, an inquest was held by Mr. T. E. Sampson upon the body of James Houghton, fifty-four years of age„a foreman platelayer in the employ of the Cheshire Lines Com- mittee, who .resided at 36, Lunt's-heath, Farnworth, near Widnes, and who was fatally injured on the railway near Farnworth, on Sunday morning. From the evidence it appeared that the down line il3e- tween Farnworth East and Farnworth Station sigilal boxes was on Sunday in course of being relaid. The traffic was accordingly worked at this point On the up main line. Deceased was walking on this lino for the purpose of getting a spade when he was knocked down by the engine of the 9.23 a.m. express front Manchester to Liverpool. The train was proceeding slowly at the time, having been stopped a little dis- tance previously in order to get from the down line on to the up main line. The whistle of the engine was also blowing all the time. The driver of the engine saw the deceased a few seconds before he was knocked down and although he at once applied the brake the accident could not be avoided. The injured man was removed to the Northern Hospital, Liverpool, where he died the same day.

A verdict of "Accidental death " was returned, the jury expressing the opinion that no blame whatever was attached to the officials.

Mr. Frederick Simpson, chief superintendent, of the Cheshire Lines Railway police, represented the railway company.

1 EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

ORG AN APPOINTAI ENT.

Mr. Charles H. Dawson has been') appointed organist and choirmaster of Wargrave Parish Church. There were twenty-five candidates for the post. ANOTHER CASE OF SMALL-PDX AT EARLESTOWN.

On Monday morning another case of small-pox was rep.rted to the Newton-in-Makerfleld Medical Officer of Health, the patient being Mrs. Metcalf, aged 28, of 59, Crown-street, Earlestown. Mrs. Metcalfe visited some relations living at Old Swan, Liverpool, on August Bank Holiday, from where a case of small-pox had been removed, and it is supposed that she contracted the disease there. This is the second case vvhiCh has occurred in the district, the first having broken out as far back as last January. In all probability the patient will be removed to a temporary hospital (a marquee) erected for the last case, but never used. The sanitary authority is endeavouring to get the patient removed to an isolation hospital at one of the surrounding towns, but it will be remembered that on the last occasion when a case of small-pox broke out attempts, were made to get the patient in hospital but without success. The public vaccinator(Dr. Valentine) advises immediate re-vaccination for all people who have not been vaccin- ated for some time past.

===== 0002601_19030829_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
PASSIVE RESISTANCE.

WARRINGTON CITIZENS' LEAGUE. Passive Resistance Meeting to be Held.

A league under the above title has been formed in Warrington with Mt. N. Balmford as president, and Mr. W. Chatworthy as secretary and treasurer. The objects are (1) To offer passive resistance to the Education Act of 1902; by refusing to pay that portion of its rate i which sto be used for privately managed schools, especially those which are sectarian. (2) To unite for mutual support and encouragement all those who on religious, moral, or national grounds refuse to pay the aforesaid rate. (3) ro provide legal and other assist- ance for members of the League suffering in conse- quence of their passive resistance to the Act. Only direct ratepayers can he full members of the League and they are asked to pledge themselves to refuse to pay that portion of the Education rate which is ta be applied to privately managed and sectarian schools.

Associate members will be enrolled of indirect rate- payers and others who, for various reasons, cannot offer passive' resistance, but will further the objects of the league to the utmost of their ability. A public meeting in support of Passive Resistance will be held in the Baptist Chapel, Golborne-street, on Thursday evening next at 8 p.m. Mr. N. Balmford (president_ of the Warrington Citizens' League) will preside. The speakers will be the Rev. J. Hirst Hollowell, of Rochdale, Rev. J. F. Matthews, of Latch- ford. The following have signified their intention of being present :—Revs. W. Mason, A. G. D. Gee, and H. Du rin ico.

===== 0002601_19030829_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
Six Cases at Fratisham.

At the Frodsham petty sessions a. large number of sympathisers with the passive resistance movement were in court. The overseers had summoned the follow- ing for non-payment of rate :—William Booth, Thomas Earlain, Noden, George E. Firth, the Rev. J. W. Mold, all of Frodsham. ; and John Noden, of Helsby. Mr. Booth said he thought the education rate was a flagrant injustice and the Act a wicked instrument to thrash people with. He conscientiously objected .to pay.—The Magistrates' Clerk: It is not a court of conscience, unfortunately.—Mr. Noden felt that he could do nothing but take his stand, play the man, and take the consequences.—The Rev. John W. Mold, Nonconformist minister, said that he could not be a party to propagation of either Anglicanism, or Romanism or any other "ism," or to a great injustice to thousands of Nonconformist teachers. The overseers, he added, had been very conciliatory in the matter.—The magis- trates ordered the defendants to pay the rate and costs within seven days. if they did not their goods would be distrained.

===== 0002601_19030829_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
Citizens' League for St. Helens.

The Rev. J. Hirst Hollowell spoke on Thursday night at a meeting of St. Helens Nonconformists on the present position of the education question. For the first time in our history, he said, Anglicanism, Ritual- ism, Romanism, and all kinds of sectarianism were a charge upon the Municipal Budgets of this country. It was decided to form a Citizens' League for St. Helens.

Further Sale at Altrincham.

The sale of goods seized from the three passive re- sisters, who were summoned at Sale on Monday, took place o Thursdayat Altrincham, at the auction-room of Mr. Ernest Walliden. The first articles pat up for sale were a'gold watch and scarfpin taken from the house of Mr. Jesse Haworth, Green Walk, Bowden,a justice of the peace for Cheshire. They realised £3 10s. Six chairs taken from the house of Mr. Charles Pidduck, also of Bowdon, were sold for £2. The last lot comprised a brooch which had been taken from the house of Mr. Thomas Percival Strong, Park Terrace, Southern-road, Ashton-on-Mersey, and it was knocked down for 365. At the close of the auction, Mr. Pidduck said he desired to thank the police and the auctioneer, from whom he had received the utmost courtesy and consideration. The proceedings were very orderly throughout. The Rev. S. P. Crarke (Crewe) moved a strongly- worded resolution denouncing the Education Act, and delivered a stirring speech.—The Rev. R. W. Roberts (Crewe) seconded. He also delivered a passionate speech, and said the resolution was too mild for him. He would like to have seen a copy of the Act publicly burned, to express their hatred and contempt of it and those who had framed and passed it.—The Rev. R. Hughes, of Alsager, supported the resolution, as did also Mr. Hampden-Cook, and it was carried.—Votes of thanks were passed to, and cheers given for,, the local rate collecter (Mr. A. Wood) for his courteous discharge of unpleasant duties, and the local auctioneers for refusing to conduct the sale.

Auction Sale at Sandbach.

The public sale of six passive resisters' goods at Sandbach took place on Tuesday afternoon. The Market Hall was crowded. The local auctioneers having refused the task, Mr. Reuben Clough, of Nantwich, wielded the hammer, and though at first he was hooteda little, the utmost good humour prevailed afterwards. All the goods were" bought in" for the resisters by the Rev. E. Hampden-Cook (secretary of the local Free Church Council), Mr. Newton. and Mr. Jones. After the sale an indignation meeting was held, and the passionate speeches several thnt.s created an uproar. On the whole, however, the gathering was orderly.

A Note of Warning to Passive Resisters.

To the Editor of the Manchester Gusrdian.

Sir,—The clerics, rating authorities,, overseers, and assistant overseers are getting greatly alarmed about the growing resistance to the iniquitous Education Act of 1902, and are adopting all kinds of dodges to keep "passive resisters " from appearing before the magis- trates for the non-payment of the school rate. Will you allow me to open the shutters and pull up the blind and pull down the upper sash so as to allowthe honest daylight and wholesome air in upon the dark doings of some of these gentry? One of the tricks that is being played upon passive resisters is for the rate collector to pretend that he is authe:ised to take the poor rate minus the school rate, and when the unsuspecting passive resister, taking him to be an honourable truth-speaking man, pays him the part of the rate whichhe can conscientiously the assist- ant overseer gives him a receipt for the whole amount and tells him the school rate portion has been paid by some third party who is sci ignorant and vulgar-minded that he cannot understand or appreciate a Nonconform- ist's conscientious objection to paying for the teaching of sectarianism under the name of religion in rate- supported schools. lam a "passive resister," and when the assistant overseer of the Culeheth district called upon me for my rate on the 21st of July last I proffered him the rate minus the education portion. He refused to take it,and told me he would have to consult with his overseer. He came again last-Tuesday evening, and on his assuring me he was now empowered to take the poor rate minus the school rate I paid him. He then gave me a receipt, and when I looked at it I found it was for the whole amount.. When I demanded a receipt for what I had paid him, he told me my school rate had been paid for me,but he would not tell me by whom, and then he drove off. I have:sinee sent him the receipt" by post and asked him to amend it. Have Ino remedy for this trick that had been played upon me? Of course, if I had suspected him of any roguery I would have asked to see the re- gelpt he intended to give me before paying him. The mord is then, " Passive resisters, demand to see the receipt before paying your rate."—Yours, W. MASON (Unitarian Minister.) Culcheth, hear Warrington.

ST. HELENS.

L &HOUR REPRESENTATION AT Sr. HELENS,

The St. Helens Trades and Labour Council held an adjourned meeting on Saturday to consider further the matter of selecting a Labour candidate for the borough. So far two candidates have been nominated for the final selection—namely, Mr. T. Glover, J.P., miners' agent, and Mr. J. A. Seddon, of the Shop Assistants' Union. Efforts are, however, being made to obtain the consent of a well-known Labour leader to become the official candidate of the National Labour Representation Committee.

DF.SERTION• OF CHILDREN.

Catherine Cullen, of Bold-street, was at St. Helens on Tuesday prosecuted by the Guardians for deserting her three children.—Superintendent Relieving Officer Thompson said the woman and her family had during the last four years cost the Guardians .C9B. On April 5, 1902, she went into the workhouse with three chil- dren, and said she was married to a man named Connelly, but when Connelly was summoned she admitted that she was not married to him. Affiliation orders for 6s. per week were made against Connelly, but the woman would not enforce the orders. In December last she was given two days' leave in order to find the man and recover the money, but she ran away.—She was sent to gaol for three months with hard labour.

THE BENEFITS OF SCIENCE.

It is seldom that any discovery of science is such as will benefit all classes of people. Some of the greatest discoveries of the age, while they have proved directly oeneficial to thousands, have also, at least for a time, injured other thousands. Even some of the best medi- cinal preparations, however much they may do to remove one class of disease, or suit some constitutions, at the same time either deepen the hold of another disease, or leave it entirely untouched, or if they benefit one class of patients, they may injure another. Now the great need of the age is some scientifically arranged preparation which will cope effectually with the prevalent diseases of this country, which will be certain to do good when fairly tried, which will be equally adapted to the needs of the mer- chant and the workman, the professional man, and he who wins his bread by the sweat of his brow, the student, the clerk, the factory hand, the miner, and the roadside labourer. It should be, too, such a preparation a contains no injurious ingredients, and which may be taken with impunity by the weakly child, or the delicate lady, as well as by the stronger constituted man. Such a discovery would deserve to be called The Perfection of Medicinal Preparations, and would indeed be an Invaluable Boon to Suffering Humanity. Now this much needed boon has been found, has been tried, has been proved, and wherever proved, it has been found satisfactory. We refer to Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters, the Vegetable Tonic, which when once tried, has been always reboinmended, and has proved successful when all other medicine has failed to give rerief, and we may say further, that it has proved permanently beneficial, when other pre- parations at best only gave temporary relief. It strikes at the source of the Disease, removing the cause of the illness, and strengthens those parts as the system which have been weakened 'by it, and therefore most liable to colds and other ailments. Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters being purely vegetable, it isa Natural Tonic, and suited to all ages at all seasons of the year. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

SCENE ON A TRAM CAR-CONDUCTOR ASSAULTED.

There was a lively scene on a tramcar at Warrington on Saturday night. the sequel being that Thomas John- son, who resides in Liverpool-road, appeared before the magistrates at the Borough Police Court on Monday, charged with having been drunk and disorderly in Liver- pool-road on Saturday night and with as3aulting Edward Kenwright, a tram conductor—Kenwright stated that defendant got on the car which left Rylands-street for Sankey Bridges at 6.10. He insisted upon standing on the conductor's platform where passengers were not allowed to stand when there was room inside the car, -and on being spoken to refused to leave the platform. When the car arrived at Green-street, witness couldsee that defendant was likely to cause further disturbance and asked him to leave the car. CWendant would not do as requested, and wlcien witness got hold of him to put him out defendan't struck him in the face with his fist, causing his mouth to bleed. But for the assist- -an:le of the driver and some of the passengers, he believed the defendant would have caused him serious injuries. The passengers were alarmed,and several ladies got oat of the car.—P.C. Wood said he was called to the car at seven o'clock, by the conductor, and he took -charge of the defendant. When the defendant got' near his own home he wanted to fight witness, and said that if he (the officer) ruled Sankey Bridges he was not going to rule him. The defendant took his coat, collar,and tie off, and witness was obliged to lock him up.—Defend- ant said he had been a teetotaller for two years, but he "broke down," and the drink he had "got into his head." He did not know what he was doing on Saturday night. —The Chief Constable said the defendant had been a respectable man until now.—A fine of 403. was inflicted.l

ROBBED HIS PATH ER.-A WORTH LESS IiVA IN OTOS

CRARACTER.

A young man, well known to the pol ice, named Patrick Richard Moran, was brought before the Bench at the Warrington Police Court on Wednesday' charged with stealing two hand-leathers, value Is., from the house of his father, William Moran, in Scotland-road.— The Chief Constable said the articles were the property of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd. About 4.30 on Tuesday afternoon Superintendent Monaghan and Detective Meacock were on duty in the neighbourhood of Tanners-lane when they saw prisoner coming along with something in his pocket. The Super- 111V:indent. knowing his character, asked the prisoner what he had, and Moran replied: "Only two hand leathers." The Superintendent found they were appar- ently two valuable hand leathers stamped with the name of Pearson and Knowles: They were used insome process of work carried on at the forge. Prisoner said he got them from his father's house. He was locked up and his father sent for. The latter told a pathetic story, and a scandalous one so far as the prisoner was concerned. He said the leathers were entrusted to him by the prosecutors as tools for his work, and, having been put on another job, he took them home until he required them. The father was perfectly honest, buthe said his son stole them from the cupboard. He said his son regularly did like that, and even stole the food that he (the father) ought. to have. Prisoner would not do anything but thieve and loaf about. This was his 20th appearance, and he had served several terms of imprison- ment for theft.—The Bench said the prisoner was a nuisance to the community, and he would be sent to gaol for three months.

A WARRINGTON APPRENTICE AND HIS EMPLOYER.

Yesterday at the Warrington Police Court, William John Twiss. Wilderspool-road, brought an action against Thomas Henry Burke, 13. Miller-street, an apprentice in tis employ to recover £8 damages alleged to have been sustained in consequence of the defendant leaving work without just cause.—Mr. Crompton represented Mr. Twigs and said defendant's apprenticeship would not expire until May, 1904. He left his work without -prmission on July 6th and had never returned. De- fendant appeared to have been at cross-purposes with his enployer in consequence of a County Court action which he brought against complainant for injuries re- ceived. The case Was stopped by the Judge and jury after plaintiff had ,given evidence.—Defendant stated that on the date in question he was following his em- ployment when complainant's father asked him', to da some " ignominious" work which he Was not supposed to do. Witness refused to do the work and Mr. Twiss told him to leave the place and go about his business. Mr. Twiss left the shop to bring the indentures but op returning said he could do nothing because his son was away. The following morning he was asked to return to work but he said he would not until some understanding was arrived at. The same night his father saw the complainant who said he would return the indentures.—The chairman (Mr. H. Roberts) said that if this statement were correct the question was whether complainant's father had not broken the indentures by asking defendant to do something contrary to the terms of the indentures—Mr, Crompton . submitted that defendant had no ground for leaving work. Mr. Twiss's father was not a partner in the business.—Defendant's father said he saw complainant, who said he would -give him the indentures. When witness saw him later he said he •could do nothing until he had consulted his solicitors. The matter remained unsottled and witness received a' bill from complainant's solicitors for L 35 185. 6d. costs in the recent action. His own solicitor's costs were £27.- The bailiffs were sent to his house to distrain for ,complainant's solicitors' bill, and later witness received -a letter charging his son with breaking into complain- ant's shop and stealing his tools. This was untrue.— Mr. Twiss stated that he promised to give defendant's father the indentures if he would pay the taxed costs of the recent action.—Dofendant's father denied this.— The magistrates ordered defendant to return to work and par costs. They did not order him to pay the, -damages claimed.

The Examiner, Saturday, August 29, 1903.

11b. G 13drrns. ; F. Blyle, 111. 2ozs., and 19 others of lesser weights ; the gross catch being 101bs. 2ozs, 51ruis. Messrs. J. Minshall and G. Spencer had charge of the arrangements, and fulfilled their duty with satis- faction. The party returned home well pleased with their day's outing. TheY prizes will be distributed on the 31st.

The se;.•,oad coznoatitor on the list was fortunate to capture a pike 161- inches long, with maggot, the peculiar feature being that the fish is covered with a bright green colour, more especially about the mouth and along both sides of the body. It is evident the fish has existed in some peculiar surroundings to pro- duce such an effect, which is not merely superficial, and certainly is not of a. poisonous nature, as it gave the captor excellent sport for some time, on the fine tackle he was using. The fish :a in the hands of the Secretary of the Association, who is preserving it for the inspec- tion of anglers.

The Committee of the Association met last Friday evening and passed the first stage in the preparation of the Year Book for 1904. The contests in connection with that body were fixed for July 13th, August 15th 17th and 24th, September 7th and 14th, all of which will take place in the Bridgewater Canal. Will secre- taries of clubs please note and arrange their fixtures accordingly, acquainting the Secretary of the Associa- tion not later than October 10th.

Of general angling during last week, not the brightest of reports are furnished. Trafford Brook which owing to the rain of last,week has attracted a large number of anglers anticipating some sport among the coursefish,was entirely off colour only a few eels falling to the share of lithe visitors. The Shropshiro Union Canal in the lower length also disappointed our devotees,but Waver- ton way matters assumed a better turn and soma sport was realized. Rossett shared also the general depres- sion, only Mr." J. Alcock doing anything worth noting by the capture of a bream of about 2lbs. The North Staffordshire Canal did not realize the anticipations of visitors to the Acton Bridge district, Mr. A. H. Mather being the only one to report sport of a satisfactory character. Lyrnm Old River has not yet returned to the form of last ye,r. The River Bollin has scarcely recovered from the 'recent floods, but several nice cap- tures of trout and course fish are chronicled.

The Bridgewater Canal at vArious points did not turn up quite the trump of previous weeks,though several reasonable catches have been recorded notably by E. Hart, who at two or three visits secured 28 roach of fair size, a large number of gudgeon and two eels. H. Martin did fairly well, also P. Leicester and J. Oultram.Reports have reached me of anglers' tackle being smashed up with little ceremony by fish of unusual pro- portions. These are evidently king carp, with which the Worseley length of the canal was liberally stocked some years ago, and which from their rapid growth, must by this time, have reached large dimensions, in proof of which I am informed, one was taken from the canal dead on the 23rd inst., which weighed 4pbs. and had evidently been killed by one of the tug propellors. so sight line fishers must not be surprised if disaster befalls them if they encounter these strong and very game fish.

On the 24th Messrs. J. W. Boyle visited the Gosvy at Tattenhall-road and found the river in grand trim for fishing, about 161 b of roach and dace being the result of the day's sport. The rumours of poisoning in this river, must not have affected the district under notice.

The Izaac of old laid clown the axiom that angling was the contemplative man's recreation,and that this isa truisim many .4 its devotees will verify. In the slack moments of the sport, under solace of a pipe, the angler's thoughts wander into various channels,his occu- pation, his home life, his record captures of fish, and prospective catches claming hislattention. The sciences also occupy no little share of his meditation, particu- larly that of botany, which he will enter into with a 'zest that would do credit to the most accomplished professor, and will wander afield in pursuit of his favourite theme for the time being. An illustration of this was related to me a few days ago. A devotee of our craft was Rhewn a very useful specimen of herb (that he was not previously familiar with) which aroused his attention at once, and naturally a minute inspection must take place, but alas the aims and aspirations of mortals, one insecure step and—a beautiful header into the stream ; a spluttering object was assisted to the bank, and once upon terraffirma,the impression evidently prevailed that botanical research sometimes has a damping effect.

The General Wolfe Club and the Ben Brierly Club of Manchester each fish their contests near Thelwall on the 29th inst, and with fine weather,no doubt good sport will be realized.

The Broadheath Angling Society also fish their annual contest in the Bridgewater Canal. Several capital prizes have been promised, and the event is looked forward to by the members.

Victory once more! After an exciting, match the Warrington eleven ran out winners on Saturday by the narrow margin of three runs. This is really something out of the usual course of events, and comes as an acceptable break in the monotonous series of unsatisfactory results which have had Who recorded of late. Heaton Mersey were the opposing team, and had to admit defeat on their own ground.

The home eleven had first turn with the bat and had compiled 91 when the last wicket fell. Warring- ton just manao-3(1 to secure three more runs than their opponents. J. A. Water Polo.

St. Heleas and Wigan met orie,e more in the St. Helens Baths on Tuesday night when the English champions of last year had to defend their title against St. Helens who had in the same bath a fortnight before dis- possessed them of the Liverpool championship. St. Helens turned out the regular team. About 400 people travelled from Wigan, and the visitors were strong in the, belief that the presence of Kearsley in their ranks would turn the scale. The result of the meeting was a brilliant game. At half time each team had placed one goal to its credit. St. Helens quickly attacked on the resumption. Final result: St Helens 5 goals, Wigan 2 goals. Wigan are thus put out of the national c =petition.

Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

The usual meeting of this Society was held on Su ii- day afternoon, when Mr. Nelson, of Warrington took the clytir, and spoke of the good work the P.S.A. was doing at Wycliffe, not only amongst themselves, but amongst outsiders as well. A Christ-like man be said was one who tried to benefit the bodies as well as the morals, and the souls of nfen.—Mr. Price, o f Alanchester, gave a very thoughful, and earnest address upon, "Shut the Gate " or the common expression "Please shut the gate." The speaker, urged young men especially to keep the gate of their lives closed to anything that would mar and spoil their better and holier feelings. He advised them first to keep the gate of their homes pure and thus keep outside those influences, that would tend to make them impure. Secondly to keep their mouths shut from impure conversation ; and thirdly to keep their ears shut, so that they could not hear, in the street, in the shop, and in the office, the foul conversations, that destroyed the atmosphere of purity around them. The meeting was taken part in by Mr. C. Cotton mid Mr. F. Brown. At the close of the first'halflyear the imember- ship is 472. The Savings Bank has just opened again and 50 members have already deposited. Mr. W. H. Payton Mus Bac. presided at the organ.

The Living of Helsby.

- The. Bishop of Chester has offered the living of Helsby, in the diocese of Chester, to the Rev. E. -W. Evans, M.A., vicar of Little Budworth' near Tarporley, and it has been accepted by him. Afr: Evans was ordained deacon in 1889 and priest in 1890 in the diocese of Chester. He was curate of Tarporley from 1889 to 1893, and has been at Little Budworth since 1898. The living of Helsby is worth £2lB gross with a house.

IN THE GREY OF THE DAWNING.

BY JOHN •K. LEYS, &uthpr of "The Lindsays," "In Peril by Night," "The Veiled Lady," &c.

"So you are Harry Selwyn ? " The young man could barely stammer out some sort of half-intelligible reply. His heart was going like a piece of machinery gone mad. He bad always heard that his great-aunt's companion was a pretty girl, but he had never expected any- thing like this. The delicacy of her complexion, the bright archness of her expression, the charming upward lift in her eyebrows, the fire in her glance, the melting tenderness of her mouth, all combined to enchant him, and to reduce him, Oxford man though he was, to a state bordering on imbecility. Selwyn would have resented the flavour of patronage in the tone if it had belonged to anyone else, but in this case it never occurred to him to do so. He would have regarded such a feeling as sacrilegious. He suffered her to lead him to a couch, still with that rather foolish look of awed surprise upon his face, while Flossie Durant was forced to turn her head away to hide the pleased smile which was curving her cheek in spite of her- self, and for which she could afford no tolerable reason.

They sat down and chatted about various things —Lady Worcester and her gout; Oxford and commemoration; the young man's impressions of London and of the War Office, in which, through the kindness of a relative, he had just obtained a post. Miss Durant said little about herself; but she left on the young man's mind the impression that she spent her days and the greater part of her nights in nursing a kind-hearted but tiresome old woman, and that she was entirely dependent for change on such brief intervals of sunshine as the present visit afforded. He considered that Florence Durant was practically a saint and martyr, as well as the loveliest being that ever put on the habit of humanity. After a time he saw reason to modify these views; but he still regarded, her as one of the sweetest and best of her sex. Before he left the house a series of communica- tions took place between Miss Durant, Lady Worcester's maid, and the nurse, with the result that Harry was admitted for the space of some eighty seconds to the sacred privacy of his great- aunt's bedroom. The old lady gave him a skinny hand, and asked what piece of folly he had been after last, told him that he might call on Thursday afternoons, warned him (quite unnecessarily, for Selwyn was a well-behaved young fellow) that if he got into debt he mustn't come to her to help him out of it, and finclly gave him a cheque for twenty pounds and bade him a curt adieu. All through the winter Harry kept getting deeper and deeper in love with the beautiful girl who lived at No. 7, Carlton Gardens' and more than once he felt convinced that he had succeeded in making an impression on her heart. • At length, one afternoon, when he had outstayed the last of the visitors, and his Aunt had gone back to her bedroom for the time, he managed to utter•the fateful words.

It was some minutes before he could get the wilful, teasing girl to take him seriously, but when she did it was only to laugh at him in a more sober way.

"Why, you foolish boy! " she cried. "How should we live and pay rent and taxes, not to speak of clothes and a hundred other things?"

"Do you love me, Flossie? Viet is the only question worth considering." "Ah! I am afraid it is not !"

"But do you love me ? " "I can hardly tell if I do what you call love you; but I like you very well, and I have no doubt I might come to like you well enough to marry you if you wished. But do be reasonable. What is the good of talking of our marrying? Your aunt is very kind to me, but 1 am only a distant cousin of hers, and I cannot expect that she should leave me anything worth mentioning. Nor can you expect to inherit a fortune from her—besides, it is ill waiting for dead folk's shoes, you know. You have your pay from the Office, and I have nothing but a fine set of rather expensive habits. Do you think we should be happy living in a forty- pound house at Clapham, or some such awful place, riding in third-class carriages, and dining (when we could afford it) off curried mutton?" She gave a little shudder. "In less than a month you would be as wretched as I, and you know it." "I don't know it ! " cried the unreasonable young man, rising from his chair. "But I don't blame you for one moment. All I know is that lam the most wretched man alive, and that I shall not see you again." "Nonsense! You mustn't say things like that. You will see me on the twentieth, at all events," said she, rising too, and giving him her hand with the sweetest of smiles.

Selwyn shook his head snd went away; but none the less the evening of the twentieth found him at his aunt's door.

It was a great occasion. The eldest daughter of the lion. Augustus Severn, Lady Worcester's favourite son, was :to come out that evening, and her grandmother had insisted that the event should take place in her house, at a ball to be given in her honour. The invitations had been just numerous enough to prevent overcrowding, and very elaborate preparations had been made. Harry Selwyn hardly knew the place. Hall and vestibule were filled with palms and semi-tropical plants, with snug recesses cunningly contrived among them. The whole suite of rooms had been thrown open, and Lady Worcester herself, blazing in diamonds, stood at the head of the flower-lined staircase to receive her guests. "She's got them all on, I see," said the voice of a smart society woma behind Harry, with a little laugh. He knew that she referred to the diamonds, which were rather famous. They were certainly worth a great deal of money. His aunt had shewn them to him one day when she happened to be in an amiable mood, and had nothing better to do; and, little as he knew about precious stones, he had been surprised at their size and brilliancy. Harry managed to squeeze his way to the spot where his aunt stood, paid the little debt that courtesy demanded, and hurried off to hunt for Flossie.

He found her in a recess, talking, or rather listening, to a tall dark man in the unifrom of a captain of some regiment. He was speaking so earnestly that neither of them noticed Harry's approach; and when Flossie did look up and see him, he could not but fancy that for an instant a frown of impatience wrinkled her brow. It was gone in a moment, however, and she awarded him a dance—a square, and pretty far down on her programme, too—with the pleasantest of smiles. Harry gallantly stood out for some- thing better, but all he could get was the some- what vague promise of an extra. With that he had to be content, and no sooner had he been dismissed than Miss Durant turned towards the Captain, who had been waiting in the background. He was at her side in a moment, and the whispered conversation was resumed. Harry felt immediately a wild, insane jealousy of the Captain. He cursed his fate that had for- bade him to follow his boyish desire of going into the army. He almost cursed Flossie for what he considered her faithlessness and want of heart; and he spent most of his time thenceforth in watch- ing the pair from afar. They danced together pretty often, he saw; and he was not the only one who observed it. Turning round once, when he was sick of gazing on the heart-torturing sight of Flussie floating round the great drawing-room in the arms of the Captain of Hussars—he had discovered that was the name of the uniform he wore—Harry caught the eye of an elderly, dis- tinguished-looking man whose bearing (though he was in ordinary evening dress) proclaimed him to be a soldier. This gentleman's looks rested on the unconscious pair with something of disapproval, and he tugged thoughtfully at his long grey moustache.

Harry, wandering about like an unquiet spirit, found himself in the supper-room, and as he stood looking moodily on his glass of champagne he heard some one behind him say in low, earnest

"I tell you it is a dead certainty. Jack Tar cannot be beaten by any horse in the field. His owner is one of the most cautious and knowing men on the turf, and lie has backed the animal for all he is worth. I have done the same, and I advise you—well, I never give advice, hut I think if you follow my example you won't be a loser." "Jack Tar is a rank outsider, I believe," said the second speaker. "You can pt thirty to one without much diffi- culty."

"And you say it is a certainty ? "

"I've been a racing man for twenty-two years, and I never knew such a chance happen before." The two men moved away, and as for Harry, he had been so much interested in what they had been saying that he had quite forgotten that he was not justified in listening to their conversation. Seeing a friend standing at a little distance, Harry went up to him, and asked him whether he knew either of the two men who were at that moment leaving the room. "I know the old gentleman by sight well enough," said Harry's friend. "He's Major Clements.— greatest racing authority extant." "You—you think his opinion is worth some- thing, then? " "Worth something! Do I think a million sovereigns worth something? The man is never wrong. He might be a millionaire ten times over if he wished. I'd give ten pounds gladly for the mere mention of a horse's name from his lips, and I'll bet you wouldn't find ten men in this house who, if they knew anything about racing, would not sell their mother's diamonds, and put the proceeds on his selection in one lump! " And in his enthusiasm the speaker drained another half-tumbler of champagne. Harry Selwyn turned away with a strange sensa- tion of being on the eve of a crisis. Here, at all events, was a swift and tolerably sure means of making one's fortune. At thirty to one—but Harry suddenly remembered that he had but a remnant of his last quarter's salary left. The advice, valuable as it might be to those who had money to risk, was all but meaningless to him. It might bring him in a few pounds, perhaps a few hundreds; but for him to win any sum which would materially alter his position, with his present resources, was quite out of the question. It was maddening to think that if he had but two thousand, even one thousand, pounds' he might gain enough to enable him to make a home for Florence, and not have the money! He went back to the large drawing.-room, which was being deserted for the supper-room, and seeing Flossie in a corner, he went up to her, and claimed the extra which he had been promised. The girl yielded, with rather a bad grace, as it seemed to him, but she did yield; and as they floated round the room together, while the music surged and billowed around them, the poor fellow was in a fever, a delirium, of love and despair and longing. - The waltz was over all too soon, and Harry sat 'with his partner in a shaded recess; but almost before he had time to utter a word of what was burning in his heart and blazing in his eyes, a tall form came softly up, and a hated voice, the voice of the Captain of Hussars, said blandly: "Our dance, I think, Miss Durant," and she was gone. It was when a big fat dowager, with a small fortune beaming and scintillating all over her, came into the recess and sat down beside him, that the idea flashed into his brain. It made his heart beat as though he had been gazing on a phantom of the night. The way to wealth, the way to marriage with Florence, lay open to him, if he had but the courage to seize the oppor- tunity! What could be easier than to remain behind, steal to his aunt's dressing-room (where 1 he knew her jewel-cabinet stood) in the dead of night, cut the bottom out of the cabinet, if the drawers could not be forced, leave the house with the diamonds as soon as the doors were opened in the morning, pledge the jewels for three thousand pounds, as he knew he could easily do, get some friends to help him to lay the bets with respectable bookmakers, and bring the stones back a few days later ? There was no insuperable difficulty in any part of the programme, and the result of carrying it out successfully would be that he would have in his pocket the sum of eighty-seven, or (allow- ing for one or two bad debts), say eighty, thousand pounds! The very thought was enough to make the blood leap in his veins, and his breath come in great heavy sobs, as if he had been swimming. With eighty thousand pounds he would be in a position to ask Flossie to marry him, and he felt sure that she would not refuse him, even if he were to win only half the money. It was only the necessaries of life she wanted, poor darling! The longer Harry thought over his project, the more feasible did it appear to him. Even if the worst should come to the worst, lie said to him- self—if he were to be detected in the act of taking the stones, or if by some unforeseen calamity Jack Tar were not to win the Cup—he felt sure that his aunt would not prosecute him. For the credit of the family she would spare him; and if the thing came off, he would bring the diamonds to her and confess what he had done for love's sweet sake.

In the end the young madman succeeded in persuading himself that this particular bit of felony was rather a fine thing to do. It proved that a"great and unreasoning passion had mastered him, and that, as all readers of modern novels know, is the very apex and flower of morality. At all events Master Harry, being excited with love, wine, and the gold 'hunger, which is a more fruitful source of crime than land-hurrger itself, yielded to the ternptatien. He retreated to an out-of-the-way corner, from which he could hear the continuous noises caused by the departure of the guests. After a time these noises ceased, and Harr!, knew that they had gone. Soon perfect silence reigned, and he felt sure that everybody had gone to bed. Then he came out of the closet in which he had been hiding, and sat down in the dark and empty ball-room to wait. An hour passed, and he judged that it was time to act. Already the grey of the dawning was stealing in through the long windows. He took off his boots and boldly went upstairs. There was no need to tread softly. His shoeless feet made absolutely no noise on the thick carpets. In a few minutes be was at the door of his aunt's dressing-room. He paused and listened, then, softly turning the handle, he pushed open the door, and crossed the threshold.

All was still. From his aunt's_ bedroom on the right there was not a sound. The two rooms were connected by a door, across which there hung a thick, heavy curtain. Between this door and that opening upon the corridor by which Harry had entered there stood a bed, and its hangings were in the line between the two doors, so that any one entering by one door could not see a person coming in by the other without pissing round the curtains. There was a large dressing-table at one end of the room, and near it a smaller table, upon which stood the jewel cabinet from which he had seen his aunt take the brilliants. The •dawn had not come yet, but it was near at hand, and light enough came through the blind for Harry to make out these various objects. He was on the point of stepping forward to the table which held the jewel-cabinet, when a slight noise, very slight, but yet perceptible, fell on his ear. It came from his aunt's room, and with beating heart he retreated to the shelter of the bed hangings.. He could not conceive how the trifling noise he had made in opening the door of the dressing-room could have awakened any one, but he reflected that perhaps his aunt, or her maid if she happened to be at hand, had been lying awake, and if so she would of course be alarmed by hearing the door of an adjoining room open in the middle of the night. As he stood and waited he heard a faint clink; it seemed to him that his aunt, or some one in her , room, was locking the door between the two rooms. But the next moment shewed that he was wrong. The door had been opened, for a tiny ray, that of a night-light, shone through the aperture, and the curtain that hung in front of the threshold was gently stirred. Horror! Some one was coming to search the apartment! How was he to explain his presence. there, at that hour? What if the maid—he felt sure it was Simmons—were to give the alarm? Should he step forward now, and beg her not to do so ?

There was no time to answer the question in his mind. Looking through a chink between two of the bed-curtains, he saw a hand push the portiere aside, and the next moment a figure clad in white from neck to ankle glided into the room. It was Flossie Durant!

- Blank surprise was the first feeling in his mind, but in the hundredth part of a second he remem- bered that there was a couch in his aunt's room which Florence sometimes occupied when his aunt did not wish to be left alone at night. Would she see him? Would she search the room; or would she be satisfied with a glance round the apartment, and go back to bed, thinking that her imagination had played her a trick? She seemed to be staring straight at him, and Harry trembled so that lie could hardly stand. To be discovered by any one in the perpetration of such a deed would be bad enough; but t 45 be caught by Florence! The idea was maddening. She was looking at him so steadily. She must see him! Not yet. Her eyes wandered from him to the other side of the room, as if searching for a hidden intruder, and Harry gently and stealthily drew back—back—towards the door, which stood ajar at his side. Two bold steps while the girl's shoulder was turned towards him, and tile thing was done. He was out of the room and retreating swiftly sown the corridor, blessing the carpet, thick and soft as a sponge, that deadened the sound of his 'eat. On. on he went, but he did not think bifa-

san unill 'ne had reached the hall, and curiel himself up on orie of the couches, as if he bsti fallen asleep there. And then, for the first time while his nerceS were yet tingling with the dread of discovery', a sense of the meanness and baseness of the sci which in his vinous and amorous excitement 130 had attempted, rose up, as it were, and struck him in the face. Ile shuddered to think of the depth of shame from which he had been saved. would have been a thief, or soinething barely dir tinguishable from one. Not wealth, not Florence herself, could pay him for contracting uuc"

stain. Rising and moving softly through the hall, hl gained the room in which the hats and coats him' been kept, and finding his ulster, put it on. Thheiii• he went back to the couch, and lay there, wilimh, to sleep, a little sad, but subdued to quietness KT the emotion through which he had just passed' $ He had lain still for half an hour, perhaps bt little more, when he became conscious of lig • rustling on the stairs behind him. Some o!i_ passing down had brushed against one of tn. flowering shrubs placed on the landing. listene Harry raised himself on his elbow and d, thinking that surely he had been mistaken. "II the rustling went on. It was coming lower lower. In the dead silence the tick of * gr3l/as father's clock that stood in a corner behind 6ra-- 1 of shrubbery, and the beating of the cold rain el the windows, sounded loud and threatening.

Silently he rose to his feet. gb oft. A slight girlish figure passed him like It was Florence ! She went on through the hall and vestibule, cii4 he heard the rattle of the heavy chain as she °loci the fastenings of the street door. The °sod startled him. Though he saw that she was (Ire wei for the street, he had not realised that she her going to leave the house. He must speak to —he must ask the reason-- The He reached her just as the door opened. Captain of Hussars stood in the portico. be • She turned and uttered a little cry 0" recognised Harry Selwyn. "Florence! BORN TO DROWN.

During the inquest on Tuesday on George Edwin Fenn, aged nine, of Bow, who was drowned in the Ducketts Canal, the Coroner, addressing the mother after she had related the circumstances of the case, said, "Why, I see this is the fourth child you have lost by drowning.—Witnan : Yes, sir. My daughter com- mitted suicide whrn she was nineteen, and two others were drowned when nine years old.—The Coroner: Why, this one is the same age.—A verdict of "Accidental death" was returned.

CANTILEVER.

I often stand upon the bridge When ships beneath go by, To view the vast expansive scene, And golden evening sky. Fresh blows the west wind from th e sea, Just as it used to blow, Beside the hills my native hills loved so long ago. In quiet shady lanes so near, Delightful walks we find, In summer when the golden corn Is dancing to the wind.

In Autumn when the trees are bare And falling leaves decay, They seem to whisper mournfnlly Ye cannot always stay. Though sweet the dawn of early spring And summer's perfumed breath, Yet in old age a kinder friend May be the angel death. In winter when the fields are white How beautiful the sky, The stream of never ending light Aud shining orbs on high.

Oh wonderful and beautiful Stupendous and sublime' No wonder that we yearn to live Beyoud the bounds of time.

S. KENYON

Aug. 1903.

MODEST BROTHER, ARTHUR

At a meeting of Aston Liberals on Monday, Mr. Arthur Chamberlain was invited to become president of the Aston Liberal Association. Interviewed on Tues- day Mr. Chamberlain said he had not yet received the official invitation.but if invited,—and his name as presi- dent would help the association,—he would accept, but he did not want titles and positions if the assurances of support were sufficient. What I want," said he, "is to see a good Liberal returned *for Aston, with sound views on the education question, temperance, and' the trade.'

FOOD AS THE PRICE OF EMPIRE.

[BY A COUREaPONDENTJ

iv is sometimes necessary to remind Mr. Chamberlain that the British Empire is a neither his sole property nor his exclusive charge. The same is true of many British citizens, who have just as little riJ as the Colonial Secretary to claim .the Esr pire their own. We do not possess the ; we are members of it. The slum dwelii-4. in East London; the farm labourer on the Welds of Lincolnshire, the crofter in the Highlands of Scotland, the Connaught peasant in the West of Ireland, and the Welsh dalesman,have all the same claim upon the Empire as a statesman in Downing-street. That claim is the right of citizenship in the federation of free and independent nations speaking the English language. The Empire has become strong; its separate States have become powerful; its citizens have become prosperous. This happy state of affairs has been brought about because we have not claimed the various parts of the Empire as our own to do what we like with,and have allowed the British love of independence and liberty to develop wherever we have planted the Union flag. The Empire, like the kingdom of heaven in one respect at least, has come without obser- vation, by natural and free growth. It has not been forced into its present healthy condition by artificial methods. Each State has developed according to its own ideas and re- quirements. Comparatively speaking, no one part has become a burden upon the others. Each has learnt to work out its own salvation in the manner best suited to its special circumstances. The bond of union between the Mother Country and• the Colonies has never become a fetter. Hence the union of the British people beyond the seas has grown stronger with years; mutual independence has created mutual regard. A century of industry and freedom has built up a great federation of British races.

Bur an Empire resting upon such a federation as this is not good enough for Mr. Chamberlain. It may have been good enough for a century of British and Colonial states- men, for the men who, like Lord Durham, first conceived the policy of Colonial inde- pendence as a means of Imperial unity. But the Lord Durham of those days is dead; so his policy is old and probably musty! Mr. Chamberlain wants to alter this policy and bring it up to date according to the Birming- ham standard. The Empire should be run on modern business lines. He tells us that the only sure and safe bond which will keep the Empire together is that of trade. Apparently that trade should be organised on the exclusive principles of a. Birmingham screw factory. Mr. Chamberlain is, of course, the man to show us how to do it. According to him, the Colonies will only remain a part of the Empire so long as the family connection means good business, and no longer. "There- fore," says he, "the working man must let us tax his food so that we can make it worth while for the Colonies not to disown the Mother Country." Mr. Chamberlain told us in the House of Commons on June 17th:

FISCAL UNION WAS THE NECESSARY BEGINNING OF FEDERAL UNITY, WITHOUT WHICH THE EMPIRE WOULD FALL INTO DISJOINTED ATOMS. To provide this "Fiscal union" we must, as Mr. Chamberlain said in the House of Commons on May 28th, "put a tax ,on food."

"IF you want to keep the Colonies ybu must pay them to stop " is, in brief, Mr. Chamberlain's idea of the only way the Empire can be kept together. Thus we see that the old bonds of kinship, common blood, common language, a common flag, and a common loyalty to the Throne are to be counted as nothing when weighed in the balance with Birmingham "business." It is hardly necessary to point out that such a disregard of all that Britons, whether at home or abroad, have considered sacred involves at once a slander upon the loyalty of the Colonies and an intolerable burden upon the British worker. Let us briefly consider what Mr. Chamber- lain's policy means. What does it mean to the worker ? If Mr. Chamberlain taxed all other

foods at the same rate that his friends say be will tax corn, viz. 2s. a quarter, it will cost the country 222,060,000 a-year. According to Mr. Chamberlain's estimate, the working classes would have to pay £16,500,000 every year to bribe the Colonies to remain within the Empire. It is estimated that the addi- tional cost of food alone would be for every working man and his family not less than £3 a year, and in many cases far more. An increase in the price of food must necessarily be followed by an increase in the cost of clothing, of house-rent, and of everything the worker has to pay for. But we cannot expect that Mr. Chamberlain or his friends would long be content with a duty of 2s. a quarter on corn. He has talked about a substantial duty on corn, and we can hardly reckon any- thing less than ss. a quarter " substantial. ' If Mr. Chamberlain's food-taxes at the rate of 2s. a quarter cost a working man £3 a year, what will it cost him if the rate is put at ss. a quarter I' And how do we know it wo,uld stop at ss. ? The probability is that once the workers of this country consented to have their food taxed. the duties on food would increase here as they have increased in France, from is. in 1880 to 12s. in 1902.

I NEED not remind you that dear food would not be the only peril the workers would have to face if they returned Mr. Chamberlain to power. In Great Britain, whenever food has been dear, other things have been dear too; work has been scarce and wages tow. But even if things were to remain as they are; even if wages were to do what wages have seldom or never done before when food is taxed —increase instead of decrease—we cannot

afford. to tax our food because of the large ' number of men and women who are constantly out of employment, and the still larger number who never have regular work. It has been estimated ttat there are 12,000,000 work- ing people in this country—more than twice the population of London—who either do not earn a "living wage" or barely earn it. What would an increase in the cost of food mean to these Nothing less than starvation, the prison, or the workhouse. "There are millions of people," said the Duke of Devon- shire not long since in the House of Lords, "for whom the margin between themselves and famine is very slender." But if we are to follow Mr. Chamberlain's plan we shall force starvation upon the poorest of the poor, the helpless hopeless, and homeless. The Briton, be he rich or poor, is patriotic. He will make sacrifices for his country, and give his life for her service. But an Empire which relies for its maintenance upon taxation wrung from the very blood of its weakest citizens is doomed to early destruction.

THANK goodness the Empire does not depend either upon Mr. Chamberlain or his policy. The natural forces upon which it has grown and prospered will continue in operation so long as they are not interfered with. Scarcely arybody but Mr. Chamberlain, in this country, has any desire to launch upon that suicidal scheme of his. It is very doubtful whether any large section of the Colonists are very auxious about the matter,,, Australia, which would gain comparatively little by a tax upon the British workers' food, is . notorioUsly indifferent. The Canadian House of Commons has passed a resolution in favour of Home Rule for Ireland as a means of strengthening the Empire, but it has not as yet asked the British working man to tax his food for the same reason. Canada recognises that the Empire can only be made stronfrAr bv thp

THE FREE CHURCHE&7I

THE CAMPAIGN OF TILE AUTUMX,

Events in the political world are moving with rapidity, and all signs point to the near approach of a General Election. In Free Church circles it is strongly held that this election—whatever the desires of Mr. Joseph Chamberlain—must not be fought on the fiscal policy of the country, but on the EduCation Question. The Colonial Secretary is no doubt wise to introduce a problem of so disturbing a nature as to shift and change the grouping of parties, but he is unwise if he thinks that the country will now acquiesce in the education policy of the Government simply because it has been established. Because the Education Bill has become the law of the land its glaring injustice is not lessened; its methods of working has rather increased its gross unfairness.

THE NATIONAL FREE CHURCH COUNCIL'S POLICY.

Under these circumstances great importance attaches to the policy of the National Free Church Council. It will be remembered that at the Brighton meetings in March the Council instructed the Executive "to carefully consider and to formulate a definite educational policy to place before the country." The result of this mandate will shortly be seen. The National Council Executive, recognising the vast import- ance of the struggle on which we are now enter- ing, is laying elaborate plans to undertake a great campaign to ensure the vital reconstruc- tion of the Education Act. Meetings .will be held in all parts, the country will be flooded with literature, and the strongest efforts will be made to secure an adequate Free Church re- presentation in Parliament.

===== 0002601_19030829_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
FREE CHURCH CANDIDATES.

As regards this last point something has already been done. A short time ago the officials of the \Libeial Party approached the National Council and expressed their strong desire to secure suitable Free Church candi- dates to contest seats at the next election. Dr. Townsend, the ex-President, and the Rev. Thomas Law, the Secretary of the National Free Church Council, promptly entered into correspondence with prominent Free Church- men in all parts of the country, and, as a result, many Free Church candidates have already been secured. Of course many more are required, and it is felt that suitable Free Churchmen who have the time and means should volunteer for this work.

===== 0002601_19030829_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GRAVITY OF THE SITUATION.

Free Churchmen are gradually realising, as they never realised before, the extreme gravity of the battle on which we are entering. It is felt that it is to be a life-and-death struggle; we must either win, or be practically exter- minated politically. No efforts will therefore be spased to secure victory, by adequate pre, parations and hard work all along the line.

===== 0002601_19030829_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
MIS-ADMINISTRATION.

The two factors in the Education situation in the immediate present still continue to be the administration—or, rather, mis-administra- tion—of the Act, and the Passive Resistance movement. Of the mis-administration of the Act abundant evidences have come to light, and show that, while the Act in its conception was thoroughly bad, in its execution it is worse. Whatever may have been said for transferring the powers of the School Boards to the County and other Councils, it cannot be denied that the change has been effected with a great deal of Ir.._ Nonconformist representation. la many un el:, where the Free Churches were adequately rel.ret-ented on the School Boards, the new anthority, by reason of the election of coun- cillo:.-.; on other issues and the use of co-option, >ntains a scandalously low proportion of Non- conformists. Then of Mr. Balfour's boasted "double boon to Nonconformists " ; what degree of control is ensured in a case like that of the civ of Oxford, where, out of 121 managers, ot.ly 10 are Free Churchmen?

===== 0002601_19030829_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
LEGAL ADVICE.

Legal advice in connection with Education continues to be in request, and numbers of cases have been dealt with by the legal depart- ment of the National Free Church Council. It cannot be too widely known that the National Council is always willing to help in cases of this kind. in many instances this help has been of considPrable service, especially in negotiations with the Board of Education and other public bodies.

REV. F. B. MEYER AND GIPSY SMITH.

There are probably fe* Men in the Free Churches more popular than the Rev. F. B. 1%1 eyer. 8.A., the President-elect of the National Fr( e Church Council. The Publication Depart- mect of that body, among a number of voluthes of especial interest to Free Churchmen it is issuir,g in the coming season, are bringing out two from the pen of Mr. Meyer. No doubt they will attain a wide popularity. A volume of Gipsy Smith's favourite solos is also to be issued by the National Free Church Council, and at a popular price.

===== 0002601_19030829_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
NORTIIFIEI.D, THE CONVENTION CENTRE.

This month's Free Church Chronicle contains a well-illustrated article on "The Great Centre of Conventions." describing the work centreing at Northfield, U.S.A. Evidently it is a beau- tiful place; says the writer: "During recent years the place has grown in consequence of the establishment of the Moody Schools and Conferences. The growth, however, has not diminished the natural beauty. With a keen eye for all that is excellent in nature, Mr. Moody had no building erected that was not in harmony with the surroundings, and the whole campus, with its undulating sward and delightful trees, is in itself a charming picture."

===== 0002601_19030829_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FREE CHURCH MISSION VAN.

The National tree Church Council Mission Van. under the charge of the Rev. Jesse Mayo, has been kept busy in Norfolk during the month, and much good has resulted from the services that have been held. The rather un- seasonable weather has not greatly affected the services, and the crowds continue to hear the Gospel under the open sky.

===== 0002601_19030829_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
3IR. KELLY AND THE INQUISITIVE STRANGER,

In this month's Free Churchman the Rev. Chss. H. Kelly relates some "Humorous Side- lights of Church Life." It is natural that one with such a long acquaintance with ministerial life should have a store of anecdotes to relate, and some of them are very good. "In some parts of the country people are very inquisi- tive," says Mr. Kelly. "They are restless till they know who the strange minister is. One such man said to me in a tumble-down sort of omnibus, 'I don't know to what denomination you beloup," No, I do not think you do.' No. sir. but I should 4ike to know your name.' Yes, but what better would you be?' 'Well, sir, Ido not recognise you.' 'And I don't,re- cognise you: so let us both be thankful, be- cause, among other things, I am a prison chaplain.' Oddly enough the worthy questioner did not seem anxious for any closer acquaint- ance."

WARRINGTON BETTING CASE. Hairdresser Smartly Fined. WATCHED FROM A WINDOW.

A betting case which aroused considerable interest occupied the attention. of Mr. F. Monks and other

, magistrates at the Warrington Police Court on Monday. 1 William Mannering, a hairdresser residing at 105, , Mersey street, was summoned for frequenting and using Selby street for the purpose of betting on the llth,l2th 13th, and 14th August. Mr. Steel appeared for the defendant who pleaded not guilty. The Chief Constable stated that there were three charges against the defendant under a well-known local bylaw, which was levelled against street betting. The first information dealt with two dates, the 11th and 12th AuGust. They comprised one charge. The second information was that the defendant used Selby-street on the 13th for betting purposes, and the third information that he frequented and used the street on Friday, the 14th inst. The facts in each case were exceedingly short, and were almost identical in character. Manner- ing was watched by P.C. Clough from 12 noon to 2 p.m. lon Tuesday, August 11th—the date of the Kempton I Park meeting—and during that time he saw him in Selby-street take money and slips of paper from 13 men and pay money out 'to four men. The defendant several times took a book lor a folded sheet of paper i out of his pocket and made notes. He was also seen to leave Selby-street and enter a passage running betweenl Selby-street, Bostock- street and Green-street, and whilst he was in the passage , no fewer than 29 men went down it,but the officer was I not able to say what took place there. He was, however, I able to say that a great majority of the 29 men wrote I something on slips of paper before they went down the entry. Subsequently the defendant Mannering re-ap- peared in Selby-street. On the following dayKempton Park races were continued, and Detective Meacock and P.C. Clough together watched Selby-street neighbour- hood from twelve to two, and at 12.40 defend- ant appeared in Selby-street. Three men imme- diately went to him and handed him a slip of paper each, and on that occasion defend- ant paid several coins to one of the men and made notes. He then went to the corner of Bostock-street and paid some coins, and while the officers were watching they saw Mannering receive money and slips from 27 men, one woman, and a boy—a total of 29. One of the men , was known by name to the police. The defendant paid I money out to eight men and made notes. He opened several of the slips and placed:the coinsin his right hand pocket and the slips in his left hand pocket. The officers were all the time within a measured distance of • 42 yards from the point where they alleged the bulk of the bets were made. On several occasions on this date Mannering left Selby-street, went into the passage, and came back again. The facts of the 13th August were almost similar. It was the Keels Park and Windsor meeting, and Clough and Meacock watched from 12 noon till nearly two o'clock, and saw 17 men and two women hand slips and money to the defendant in Selby-street. They saw the defendant pay cash to seven men, and nearly all the transactions were entered in a little book or sheet of paper. Again he appeared to have gone into that mysterious passage and to have comeback again. The next charge related to the 14th August, andthat was the date of the Royal Windsor and Redoar meeting. The defendant appeared in the street about the usual time, and while the officers were watch- ing him they saw slips and .money handed to him by 14 men, one woman, and one boy. Money was also paid out to two men and one woman, and a book used as before.

P.C. Clough gave evidence as to the alleged betting on the first day.

Detective Meacock was called to support the state- ment in regard to the other charges.ln cross-examination he said the majority of the coins passed were silver, but he could not say exactly what they were. He and Clough watched the defendant through a window which was partly open. They did not look through the glass. They had opera glasses but did not require them as they could see distinctly all that took place. The Chief Constable: I measured the distance myself. It was 42 yards. The building was in a direct line from the place where the alleged betting took place and the position.was_an elevated one. _

P.C. Clough was again called and was questioned in regard to the window. He said that he and Meacock looked through the glass and did not open the window. It was, he said, opened a little on two days by the occupier of the house to let in fresh air. _ _ _

Mr. Steel, for the defence, contended that the case for the prosecution having beenclosed, it was • incom- plete inasmuch as the bylaw under which the proceedings were taken had not been7proved. He admitted that he had been unable to find any casel bearing on the point. but the editor of" The Justice of the Peace " laid it down that the bylaw must be proved in each case, and that unless it was proved no conviction could stand. The Clerk to the Magistrates ruled against Mr. Steel, and said it had been the practice of the Court to put in the bylaw to which was attached the Corporation seal. That had been done at the present case. Mr. Steel said if the bench decided against him the case might possibly go to appeal on the point he had raised. Continuing he said it was for the police to prove that the defendant had frequented and used this particular street for the purpose of betting. He sub- mitted that the evidence of two constables at 42 yards away, and who swore that they saw money and slips of paper passed between the defendant and the other people was not conclusive evidence of betting. He granted that circumstances looked suspicious, but the magistrates could not convict on suspicion. If the charge had been any other than that of betting . the magistrates would have required a much stricter proof before convicting, and if that was so with regard to one charge why should it not be so with regard to another charge? He questioned whether the officers could definitely say that slips of paper and money were passed to the defendant, because there was a material discrep- ancy in their own evidence with regard to the open window. First Meacock swore that the window was open and that ths police looked through the aperture, whereas Clough said that the winalow was not open except on two ocoasions, when it was opened only slightly to let in fresh air. There being such a discrep- ancyon a material fact he submitted that the evidence was not reliable.

The Chairman said there was no material difference in the evidence of the officers.

Mr. Steel then submitted that the magistAates could not convict on the last two informations, because going into the street for an hour on one day could not be called frequenting. As he understood it, frequenting a street meant using it often.

The Ch.aisiman : ' I shall call all this special pleading. The Chairman said the magistrates were quite satisfied that the facts had been proved but defendant could exercise the right of appeal on the technical point if he chose.

Superintendent Monaghan said that a little over twelve months ago the defendant was fined £5 and costs for street betting. in the same neighbourhood. The number of complaints the police had received about hint was enormous and he pressed the case most strongly. The Chairman said Mr. Steel had done his best for his client, but they were all quite satisfied that the cases had been proved, and that the street was being used constantly for betting purposee. The defendant would be fined £5 in each case and costs—.£l6 17s. 6d. Itogeth er.

PENKETH,

THE POSTPONED NIAVPOLE.—The Maypole dance of the children, that should have taken place on the occasion of the lowa flower show, was held on the show field on Thursday last. Twice postponed, it looked as though the weather would again prevent the dance taking plaoe, but towards evening the weather fortunately cleared up. The Penketh Tannery Band supplied the music, and the interesting and clever evolutions of the boys and girls were watched and applauded by a large crowd. The training of the children, showed much care, and they evidently enjoyed the dance immensely. BAND SUCCESS.—Last Saturday the Penketh Tannery Brass Band journeyed to Ellesmere Port to take part in a contest inaugurated by the Elles mere Port and Whitby Horticultural Society. There were ten entries and the awards of the judge were as follows: Ist, Ellesmere Port Public; 2nd, Tranmere Gleam;^ 3rd, Penketh Tannery; 4th, Crostield's. The test piece was a selection entitled "A Casket of Gems," and Mr. J. Harper was conductor of the Penketh Band. This success is very welcome, and the Band hope to achieve still greater things ere long.

THE COBDEN CLUS.

MR. HAROLD Cox, Secretary of the Cobden Club, writes:— In your issue of August 22, a Mr. Hughes accuses the Cobden Club of being a lbreign institution. I have so often publicly denied this ridiculous allegation, that. it will be sufficient if I now repeat that the statement is as untrue in fact as it is malicious in intention. Mr. Hughes also asserts that the Cobden Club is run- ning away from a challenge given by Colonel Howard Vincent. This statement is also untrue. The Cobden Club always has been and still is willing to debate with Colonel Howard Vincent, Mr. Chamberlain's policy as laid down in his speech in the House of Commons on May 28th. It is for Colonel Howard Vincent to ex- plain why he evades this very clear issue.
