OUR FISCAL POLICY.-A TAX ON FOREIGN

CORN.

MR. EDWIN WOODS writes : I notice that many of the speakers and writers in all parts of Great Britain seem to be very much alarmed at the prospect that corn com- ing into this country may in future be taxed. I have been a Free-Trader all my life, but for the last few years I have thought that we were making a great mis- take to be Free-Traders in Great Britain, when other countries shut us out by protective tariffs. After due consideration I have lately come to the conclusion that if we tax corn coming in from Ameriea, Russia, and the Argentine Repubic, to the extent of ss. per quarter, and allow our colonies to send it in free, the price of corn will not materially advance if we impose the tax in the following manner. I suggest that we tax it to the extent of 5s per quarter, starting with is. the first year,and increasing it lat Is. a year, until it reaches ss. per quarter. While the tariff is gradually being raisedagainst thecountries who do not allow us to send in our products free, Canada; and the other colonies will be able to put into cultivation gradually, an increasing quantity, and as Canada has more land for cultivation than the United States, there is no reason whatever that they should not, in the time I have stated, be able to grow and send us as much as or more than America. By this means I maintain that -corn will not advance inprice to any appreciable extent. I admit,if you were to put ss. a quarteron the countries I have mentioned above, in the first year, not doing it gradually, we should be taxing ourselves, and shutting them out before our colonies hadhad time to increase their preduction of corn ; and unless I alter my opinion, instead of remaining a Free Trader, or, to be more correct, a ene-sided trader, I should be pre- pared to do all I cGuld for the candidate who would even put a tax on cvm, if he would. vote for it to be done gradually, SJ that the thing might right itself in four or five years. Last, but not least, whilst taking this extra corn from 4tatir colonies, we should be enriching them, and by this means it would cause an expansion in these countries in railways and other developments, concerning which if we could make a working agreement with them, their demands for materials from the mother country would be enormous.

KR. W.P. RYLi NOS CLOSING WORDS.

M. W.P. RYLANDS writes Mr. Crosfleld's absence Mr. Parker,has favoured us with an explanation of the apparent discrepancylin Mr. Crosfield's figures,to which I drew attention in my last letter. This explanation only shows how unreliable statistics can be made. It ,would„never occur to me to eliminate inter-Australian Trade any more than that between Australia and Canada. The figures I gave constituted the whole of the imports of the Colonies, whether from foreign countries, from theiUnited Kingdon or from other colonies, and in estimating the amount of trade that it might be ,possiblefor this country to secure un- der a system of Prefereutial Tariffs, I made allowance for the inter-colonial trade which would no doubt be unaffected. I have the statistical abstract for the several colonial and other possessions of the United Kingdom before me,and I find that in the year 1901 the Colonies imported in round figures 100 millions from the United Kingdom, 100 millions from foreign coun- • tries, and 50 millions from other Colonies. Of the 100 millions at present supplied by foreign countries, the propertion which could be drawn from the United Kingdom must be purely a matter for conjecture. In character, the exports from the United Kingdom do not differ very materially from those from other countries, and every manufacturer who exports to the Colonial markets-experiences competition from abroad. The com- modities therefore which must be exclusively drawn from foreign countries cannot bea:large proportion of *the whole. Regarded :in this way I still adhere to my view that the amount of trade that might be secured by this country, at present lost, would be considerable, :and might wellrreach the figure of 50 millions named in my earlier letter, in addition to those other advantages to which I drew attention. Fox the moment I cannot usefully pursue this question farther. All I contend for at the present time is an open mind to consider any scheme which may be put before us, and when a scheme ' has been elaborated for our consideration I will be happy to take part in its discussion. In the meantime I must deprecate the unyielding. Toryism of men like Mr. Parker who pre-judge the iesue, and style as an ill-considered and unworkable scheme, a scheme the details of which have yet to be revealed. In closing my contributions to this correspondence I would like to quote the words of Professor Hewirs, one of our leading Professors in political econowy. "1 am clearly of opinion that the whole trend of economic method and investigations in recent times is in the direction indicated by Mr. Chamberlain,and that the breach of continuity of development would be not in abandoning, but in maintaining our present Fiscal Policy." -- MR. H. P. HUCHES ON THE HATTER AND THE BOOT MAKER. Mr. CHARLES E. PARKER writes :

I have read with interest the various lettersthat have appeared.in your paper from the pen of Mr. 11. J. Hughes. Although in no way subscribing to his con- clusions, deeming some of them at least to be based on faulty premises, 1 am glad to recognize one who thinks, reads, and takes an intelligent interest in the controversy of the hour. The great lack in these letters appears to me to be that Mr. Hughes ignores, or rather perhapsavoids, the great law of exchange. At consider- able length and detail, your correspondent has told as of the competition certain trades are exposed to by foreign imports, but he has told us nothing of the indus- tries engaged in the export trade, which are createdand which are the natural and inevitable result of free imports, nor of the home trades aided by cheap imports. In the last letter I think for the first time, there is mention of the goods taken in exchange. It is true it is contained in a quotation, cf which, by the way, Mr. Hughes' letters largely consist. But inasmuch as Mr. Hughes characterizes the essay of the author of "Made in Germany." from which he quotes as "broad and statesmanlike," and speaks of Mr. Williams as "one of the greatest of our political economists," we may take it that Mr. Hughes approves the reasoning and adopts the conclusioas. It does, however, appear to me a strange thing that a thoughtful man like Mr. Hughes cannot recognize and discard the fallacy cOntained in the following "gem of political economy" :—"lf a hatter sells a hat to a bootmaker and buys a pair of boots from one who also dwells within the same country or union you get a double exchange within the same area. Suppose the hat and the pair of boots to be each valued at 11 the wealth of the country is increased by the raftwafacture of 12 worth of goods. If on the other hand the hatter sells his hat to a boot- maker in a foreign country, and buys a pair of boots in exchange, from a foreign maker there is only one exchange and only LI worth of wealth created within the country or the territory of the union. For the pros- perity of the people inhabiting the territory, therefore it is desirable that the exchanges should, as far as possible, be made within the territory." Now let as examine and apply this "gem of politi- cal economy "to the question of our international ex- change. Let us suppose thehatter in question has been buying boots for 22sf rom a home producer,and a foreigner offers him equal value for 20s. It does not concern us here how they are produced, whether by more economi- cal manufacture, or whether it is a case of selling be- low cost, commonly called "dumping." Now in this case instead of giving a hat worth £1 and 2s. extra the hatter has only a hat value 11 to give in exchange for the boots. He has therefore 2s. ,to spare and to spend in his own country on some article labour has produced. If his production of hats for home use is say I,oooa week,and he sells one abroad, there is the extra labour produc- ing the extra hat. But what of the bootmaker who has had a pair of boots less to make because of the imported pair? Simply thiAl. That the labour employed in making a pair of boots is replaced by the labour employed in pro- ducing the exported hat. *ln the amount of labour ern- ployed there is therefore no loss its Mr. Williams mm- plies. There is the same amount of value in the country I only part is got by exchange, hut in this exchange 2s. I is gained by the hatter, and can be spent in purchasing I the productof labour in some other direction. Multiply ' this gain by the multifarious exchanges with foreign products and some idea will be got of the national gain through international trading. But it may be argued that there is an inconvenience caused to the individual bootmaker, and that it is small consolation to him that the loss he sustains is made up by the increased trade of the hatter. I grant this. Free Trade from a national standpoint I ist always profitable, although individuals may suffer

from the transference of trade. I am in entire agree- ment with Mr. Hughes that all the advantages are not on one side. It is like a game of whist, tricks will go •to both sides, but I claim that the Free Trade holds most of the trumps and always comes out right on the rubber. The inconvenience, and sometimes hardships, resulting from the changes in employment are by no means peculiar to Free Trade. They may be due to many causes, such as the introduction of machinery, change of fashion, some new invention etc. But are we like the Ludites, to smash machinery, make change of fashion illegal, and discourage invention because cer- tain individuals are inconvenienced, thereby? The test of Free Trade and every other national question is whether or not it is for the general well-being, for the good of the greatest number? This is a great national question and must be viewed from a national standpoint. Mr. Hughes in-one of his recent letters stated that the advocates of Protection were those interested in trades injured by Free Trade, and the defenders of Free Trade, naming Messrs. %V. H. Lever, A. H. Crosfield and C. E. Parker were these whose industries were not yet threatened by foreign competition. I notice in the last letter that Mr. Hughes significantly omits my name, and only repeats those a the other two gentlemen. Pro- bably he has found out that the leather trade, in which I 'am largely interested has had as strenuous a fight with foreign imports as the iron and steel trades, and this being so, it may surprise him to find that I am resolutely attached to the Free Trade policy, and prepared to do all I can to defend it. Itwould not be fitting on my part to claim for myself any greater patriotism than the advocates of a Protectionist system, but I cannot help thinking that those who view this subject solely from the standpoint of their own trade take anarrow view of the question. If it were possible to limit Protection say to the iron trade or to the leather trade, I doubt net that those industries would flourish, at any rate for a time at the general expense. It would be satisfactory if we could carry out the wish of a man engaged in the herring trade, who wrote to Sir Robert Peel saying he was a convert to the Free Trade policy in everything but herrings. But inasmuch as partial Protection is impos- sible, because it is impracticable and unjust, and if adopted all round the temporary benefit to particular trades would soon be lost by the set back to national progress, I claim—even looking at the question solely from the standpoint of individual gain,—every manufac- turer should stand by the policy which makes for per manent general prosperity.

A LONDON CORRESPONDENT AND MR. HUGHES

A LONDON CORRESPONDENT writes : I see that your writer Mr. H. P. Hughes condescended to notice my few remarks but has not ventured on an answer to them, but has written quite in accordance with the Old Bailey tradition and Jingo style: "No case, abuse the plaintiff's attorney." I notice he is again largely quoting. I suppose the Mr. Williams he is quoting from to be the one who writes in the "Windsor Magazine." If that is the case your reader will no doubt have seen his fallacies exposedlongago. I rather like Mr. Hughes' assertion "that the Free-Trade party will oatch the workingman by appealing to his stomach," especially when we consider that Mr. Joseph Chamber- lain and his followers began the attack on Free-Trade by saying that the workingman would have more money to spend, and use, and live upon. I give the following as an illustration of German "dumping" in this country. Much of the prosperity of the Tyne at the present moment is due to the cheapness of steel plates—the raw material of ship building—the low price of which is one of the chief grievances against Germany. Among the thriving factories which have grown upon Tyneside in recent years is one for the manufacture of ships' fittings. This firm is at the present moment engaged on a large order for German ships, now building or lying in German waters and makes th.esefittings out of German steel! Germany is not only sending us iron and steel but at the same time• she is sending us employment for our workers, and then buying back from us the products of their labour. The following is a list of the rapidly increasing Ger- man imports : German imports of manufactures only. 1880

1890 1895 46,000,000

• 1900 Does not this point to the factthat duties are largely a failure, so far as keeping goods out is concerned, but they unfortunately succeed in putting up the price. America has in the last three years increased its im- ports by £30,000,000. The Inland Revenue report for 1902 shows that the tales of ruin in the British iron trade are not correct. They give ironworkers' profits as follows:- 2. 1897-98 1898-99 1899-00 1900-01 1901-02 I like the letter about the Summer House. Ido not think the maker was a Warrington man. He would soon have supplied that Summer House and others too, and made a profit. There is no way I suppose of sending the wood as raw material and a man to work it up. I have heard of similar things being done and not many months ago. No. a Warringtonian would have got over the difficulty. Fleet-street could not see its way. I admit it has been difficult to follow Mr. Hughes in his papers. They have, to use one of his expressions, been more plausible than practical." They appear to have a lot of" Grub-street "about them.

MR. A. H. CROSFIELD REVIEWS the

SITUATION.

IMr. A. H. CaosFrELD,writing from St. Moritz, Switzer: land, says : _ Thank you for your most apropos quotation in your leading article in Saturday's "Examiner" from Mr. Chamberlain's speech in the 'House of Commons, where he stated that he was prepared to go into any workman's house and say: "Now this policy, if it is carried out, will cost you SO much a week more than you pay at present for your food . . . . and I will give you a table from which you can tell for yourselves how much extra wages you must get in order to cover the extra expenses of living." How does this square with the remark in hislletter to Mr. Griffith Boscawen that, "las regards food there is nothing in the policy of tariff reform which I have put before the country which need increase in the slightest degree the cost of livinc, of any family in this country "? In other words, Mr. Chamber- lain when he first announced his policy stated that it would mean an increased cost of food, and therefore increased cost of living for the whole country, the chief burden of which of course would have fallen not only on the working class, but on millions of others who have to eke out a living on slender means. Well, to put id mildly, this precious proposal did not commend itself to public opinion. There are no doubt many ramifications and complexities in this subject, but there are certain main features which are pretty well "understanded of the people" already. In the first place our Home trade is at least five times greater than our Foreign trade, and as increased cost of living means diminished power of spending, the demand in the Home trade as a whole would have been reduced. But that means less trade and less demand for labour, and in face of this how many manufacturers are . willing to promise their employees the higher wages that are to compensate Orem for the increased cost of living? I have not met with one. As regards Old Age Pensions, the" Standard" news- paper rather naively and very truly observed that this might easily have been taken for a bribe : and a bare fsced bribe it was. But the hook, even with this bait has not been swallowed. "Once bitten twice shy" and Mr. Chamberlain has juggled with this question of Old Age Pensions so often thathis promises Are now taken cam. grano sails, and a very big grain of salt at that. That this question of Old Age Pensions will have to be dealt with in the near future we know very well, but unless I am very much mistaken the operatives of this country will only entrust this difficult yet pressing and important question to those who will deal with it as such, rather than those who have simply used it for electioneering purposes. And so this pretty little plan did not catch on. The average Englishman failed to see how we are to increase our prosperity by increasing ourexnenses, and therefore Mr. Chamberlain performs a volte face with his usual dexterity and says he is not going to increase the cost of tiring at oil! No doubt'he is wise to do this, but even his most" thick and thin "supporters may well be staggered by the amazing levity with which he sets to work to pull up our whole fiscal and commercial system by the roots. It really looks as if he had attempted to think outneither theinethods nor the consequences of his desperate undertaking. And what a mockery and 1 sham it is to pretend that by reducing or abolishing taxation on tea and sugar a fair and adequate set-off to taxation on bread and mutton can he secured to the people. The" Spectator " which has written admirably upon this great issue from the commencement, has a, capital article in last Saturday's issue. Bat there is a very important, point which is not alluded to at' all in this article. Perhaps the "Spectator" ,does not approve of a "Free Breakfast Table," but that well trodden plank in the Liberal plat- form must be familiar enough to Mr. Chamberlain. How are we to get it? Not by continuing the reckless and lavish expenditure—and increase of expenditure—of the last sev n Years, but partly by reasonable economy and partly y tapping a vast source of revenue in the taxation of land values. You have allowed the to urge in your columns more than once the supreme import- ance of this reform, and it is not necessary for me therefore to trespass on your space by discussing it in detail here. Like the Fiscal controversy, it has no doubt its ramifications, but the main and vital con- sequences of such a reform are plain enough in all conscience, and while they affect the future welfare of the whole population of thelUnited Kingdom,are specially and vitally interesting to the dwellers in our towns and cities. Because for them the issue isnot onlyeconomical but physical as well. The taxation of site values in the neighbourhood of big centres of population at the value their owner puts upon them means that they will not, as heretofore, be frequently held for speculative pur- poses, but come into the market. That will give

===== 0002601_19030829_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Saturday August 29 1903

builders their opportunity—under proper supervision— and by a natural process we may come to seerealised on a large scale those garden cities which are the hope of so many of the best minds of our country. At the same time the pressure of population at the heart of our towns and cities being reduced, rents and the value of land there will fall and municipalities will have it more in their power therefore to acquire land for the widen- ing of streets and the conveyance of workpeople to and from the manufactory to the suburbs. There are many things yet to.be done to mend (in the old phrase) the health, wealth, and happi- ness of our people, but this great reform which the Liberal Party has yet to grapple with will do more in thisldirection than anything else. The mental and physical advantages it must confer upon dwellers in towns and cities are obvious enough, and I need not here expatiate upon them; for the purposes of this letter it is the economical, side of the question that most concerns us. Taxation—just and adequate taxation—of land values will bring in a large revenue and therefore substantially reduce taxation in other ways. Then will come the turn of the "Free Breakfast Table." A Free Breakfast Table we ought to have, but not at the cost of a dearer dinner and supper table. But Mr. Chamberlain has told us we have the choice between making sacrifices or disintegrating the Empire. I imaeinei there are not many' of us either at home or in the bolonies who are unwilling to make sacrifices for the Empire. Is net England doing pretty well already in offering a free and open market for Colonial goods, and at the same time bearing the heavy burden of Imperial defence? And is it not elear the Colonies are alive to this? The conference at Montreal has made it amply clear. If space admitted one wouldlike to analyse a good deal that was said at the Conference which is of he highest significance. The real import of Sir Wilfrid Laurier's speech cannot be mistaken, and I respectfully draw the attention of your readers to it. There is one other point to which I would draw atten- tion, and which has reference to the taxation of raw matprials. Mr. Chamberlain has told us that such raw materials as wool and cotton are not to be taxed, but if we come to bargaining with the Colonies in order to get at a Preferential Tariff they will undoubtedly insist on a voice in the matter,and that also. has been proved plainly at this Conference. You have Mr. Peasoek of Hobart, Tasmania, saying that he thought there would be no harm in putting a tax on wheat in the interests of Canada; ,but why," he asked," should the Australian wool merchant not have preference as well as the Canadian wheat grower?" Then Mr. Worrell has been writing to the " Times " from Sydney assuming that "the people of Great Britain and Ireland will have to pay a little more for their food stuffs and even a few of their raw materials." It has also been suggested on behalf of Canadian timber growers that they would have an equal claim to assistance with the Canadian wheat growers. What a prospect this opens up! ! And yet, with much protectionist talk at Montreal have we heard any threats that the Empire will break up and the Colonies seek other ties unless Mr. Chamberlain'sspecifics are adopted? We hear nothing of the kind. The Colonies are natur- ally enough willing to take as much as we wish to give them, but speaker after speaker declared that nothing was clenrcttAled from the mother country. After all the Colonists are reasonable people like ourselves, and they recognise that we are already treating them handsomely, and that they have in fact the best of the bargain. In the face of this it is no use Mr. Chamberlain trying to make our flesh creep with threats of dissolution of the Empire. On the contrary one *ell understands Sir Edward Grey saying as a strong Imperialist that he "dreaded substituting the bond of sentiment for the bond of bargaia." I see Mr. Hughes in a candid, interesting and in part amusing letter has been drawing a dark picture of things as they are, and as they are likely to be if England sticks to Free Trade. I wonder if he has studied the figures given us by the Board of Inland Revenue, or if he thinks the condition of any other people in Europe un- der protective systems will compare for one instant with the condition of our own people under Free Trade. May I again ask you to extract the figures summarised for us is last week's "Spectator," and to give as muchlof the valuable comments on those figures as you have space for. I think if Mr. Hughes will kindly consider the real facts as stated so clearly and forcibly in this article it will much add to his peace of mind. I notice in his letter a reference to the Soap Trade., Well the total exports of English soap are under 50,000 tons a year, and the total imports of soap are less than a quarter of that figure, and Ido not see what is to be gained by comparing a trade of small dimensions like this, with other trades that run into so much bigger figures both in weight and value. But if Mr. Hughes means that English soapmakers do not suffer at all by the protective systems of Foreign countries, he is entirely under an illusion. Makers of machinery may said, and do send their manu- factures in large quantities to Germany, the tariff not- withstanding. But the tariff on soap shuts us out com- pletely from that market, and we are similarly shut out from other big markets such as Russia, Prance' Spain, America, Canada and Australia. But does Mr. Hughes think that by preferential trade the Canadian and Australian markets will be opened to us? On the contrary, both Canada and Australia have plainly told us they mean to continue to protect their nascent industries. Whether it is in all cases an advantage for them to do so we need not stop to consider. Let it suffice that we have to deal with that fact, which stares us in the face; and as regards the protected Continental markets, we have seen from fiscal warfare between themselves what a delusion it is to think that they will pull down their protective barriers if we begin to raise OUTS. There is another side to the question. and as Mr. Hughes has raised it I commend the following facts to his attention. There are a great many markets in the world where we have to compete on even terms with the other manufacturing nations of the world. There is China, the Straits Settlements, India, Citl)e Colony, Egypt, other parts of Al rice, the West Indies and other markets I could mention. Very well, the German ' makers who are small and the American soap makers who are large have both made a pretty stiff fight for the Isoap trade in many of these markets,and as a result of it all where do they stand now, with their trade in these markets where we compete on even terms? lain glad to tell Mr. Hughes that their share of this soap trade is a mere bagatelle as compared with the export of English soap, and th.ereason is very simple. Although unfortunately shut out from selling in some markets, we are able to buy in the cheapest and therefore our cost of manufacture is lower than the cost of manufac- ture in America, Germany, France, Russia etc.,—these countries that ,enjoy the blessings of Protection—and they are beaten in the longlrun,—notzeithstanding cer tam n rebates on materials used in manufacturing soaps for export which are allowed in come of these countries. _ _

shipping returns for 1902 are ten millions. Thirty I years before they were only six millions. The savings-bank deposits have doubled in twenty years, while those of the Friendly Societies have trebled. We cannot, however, to-day do more than just touch on thesesides of prosperity. and must be con- tent with pointing out that the Income-tax figures are an absolute and conclusive proof that we are not living on our capital and" bleeding to death," bat just the reverse. No doubt the Protectionist, driven from his " bleeding to death" position, as he must be will say that even if Free Trade has not im- poverished but enriched us, Protection would do the same, only far more quickly ;add that if we want to reach heights of prosperity yet undreamt of, we must follow Mr. Chamberlain's moving light across the fiscal morass. Strange as it may seen to the Pro tectionist, we prefer the smooth road which we are already on—a road which we know is going in the right direction—to any so-called short-cut across a doubtful bog. the only recommendation of which short cut is that it has been used by a great many foreigners. Unless we are greatly mistaken, this will also be the answer of the nation.

Rough corners to be rounded off in our prAsent fiscal systerathere may be, questions of bounties in some cases to be considered. If there are let us have these specific cases and consider them on their merits. A bona fide enquiry of that kind we are willing to listen to, but an attempt to bullyor cajole us into a disistrous Protectionist re-actionall along the line (and that is what is really being threatened) we will reiist to the utmost.

THE GROWTH OF THE NATION'S WEALTH. "THE SPECTATOR" of last Saturday says : "The most striking comment yet made on Mr. Chamberlain's policy is one not made consciously or directly, or by anj partisan, but accidently or automatically; and certainly quite imps.rtially, by a great public office. The Annual Report of the Com- missioners of Inland Revenue, issued on Wednesday, shows with a quite astonishing degree of com- pleteness the folly and fatuity of the Chamberlain proposals. If fiscal Blue-books were read by the electors, this last edition to theirnumber could not but have the most crushing effect. •It is the final answer to the plea of the Protectionists. Let us see how .this comes about. If you search the Prof tectionist plea to the uttermost—that ocean of error of which "the Chamberlain policy" is a gulf or inlet—you will find that it rests on the allega- tion that Free-trade is ruining the country. The Protectionist asserts that we are "bleeding to death." Let us turn to the figures supplied by the Board of Inland Revenue, and apply the test. The Com- missioners in their report have selected for com- parison the incomes brought under review in certain series of years.—i.e., in the years 1868-69 1875-76, 1891-95, and 1901-2. In 1868-69 the gross income brought under the review of the departmett was £398,791,000; in 1875-76 the figures were £511,376,000, an increase of £145,582,000, or 36'5 per cent; in 1894-95 the gross income was £657,097,000; in 1901-2 it was £866,993,000, being an increase of £209,896,000, or 319 per cent on the gross income In other words, then, during the period when the 6 bleeding-to-death "-and-living-on-our-capital pro- cess has been, according to the Protectionists, fully at work the gross income under the review of the Bsard of Inland Revenue has risen from £544,370,000 in 1875-76 to £866,993,000 in 1901-2. If this is living on our capital, the process is surely one which we ought all to follow. But what makes these figures the more-remarkable, as the Commis- sioners point out, is that the years with which the present state of things is compared were years of abnormal prosperity. Very interesting, too, is the table as to investments abroad. We are perpetually being told that every year more and more of our foreign investments are being thrown out to the wolves of foreign Protection—i.e., have to be sold to pay for our imports. What are the facts? A table in the Blue-book- which presents a survey of such income for a period of twenty years shows that the amount has almost doubled, rising from £31,890,000 in 1882-83 to £62,550,000 in 1901-02. Clearly there is not much "bleeding to death "going on here. We have dealt as yet only with the evidence that we are not living on our capital afforded by the Income-tax returns. If, however, as the " Standard " points out in its excellent leading article of Thursday, we go to the returns dealing with the Bankers' Clearance-house, British shipping, savings-banks, and the returns as to Friendly Socie- ties. the same story is told,—i.e., of increasing capital. The Bankers' Clearaace-house figures are nownearly double what they were in 1871. Our a

a short time, a:third of it would go abroad." By "abroad" Mr. Schwab would mean, to a large extent, free-trade The Congested State of the Hospital. Britain. The male officer will be responsible for the male bathing aad will sleep adjacent at night in case of emergency. The CLERK said the foregoing were very important resolutions and would be placed on the agenda for thel next meeting. CLARKE thought the whole matter ought to be settled at the next meeting. Mr. ASHCROFT was of opinion that the Guardians ought to see this handy-man before they appointed him. ONE-SIDED FREE TRADE v. PROTECTION. Final Lettar.

Mr. H. P. HUGHES writes :

As this is, for the'present, my final letter upon this topic, I will endaavonr to embody material broadly covering, or summarising, my previous seven letters. In a recont communication, I drew attention to the remarkable growth of Protectionist literature in this country, of late years.; ani I specially singled out for commendation, the book written by Mr. G. Byng (" Protection: The views of a Manufacturer "), pub- lished by Eyre and Spottiswoode. During the last few days, I have had an opportunity of reading another strongly-written book, entitled The Curse of Cobden and the Worship of Diana," by Mr. J. Buckingham Pope, Barrister-at-Law (London, Duckworth and Co., one shilling). This book, on its first production in 1885, rapidly reached the astonishing sale—for a book of that serious class—of over one hundred thousand copies. A 1903 edition is available, and ought to com- mand a ready sale, now that we are all trying (as Mr. Chamberlain putts it) to educate ourselves on fiscal topics. Speaking of Mr. Chamberlain, Mr. Pope says :—"I have not always been in accord with the legislation inaugu- rated, or the utterances evolved, by that gentleman; but I have no hesitation in saying, that, if he succeeds in pricking the ridiculous—but withal fatal—free-trade bubble, which has so long been regarded as solidarity by the people of this country, he will have performed a greater service for England then any Statesman who ever lived."

In my earlier letters, I quoted figures of exports and imports under sp9cific hea,Aings of goods, let me now quote, from Mr. Pope's book, some figures, set out in countries : _ _ _ _

_ _ 1 Ye-ir's Import 3 1 Year's Exports into England. from England. Russia 25,673,958 Germany 33.633,956 United States 126,961,601 France 50,612,923 Portugal 3,411,173 Spain 14,285,856 Belgium 26,538,759 Holland 34,842,528 Sweden ' 9,567,844 Norway 5,408,650 Italy 3,582,246 Denmark 15,556,780 _ _ _ _

Mr. Pope's figures distinctly prove that England is living on Capital, and that her immense riches, and the countless millions she has been able to invest in foreign Securities, are now being rapidly withdrawn in 'rder to provide for deficits arising from the operation of Free Trade. _ _ _

Leaving Mr. Pope, for the present, with a wish that many of your readers will seek a closer acquaintance with his book, and also with the book published by Mr. G. Byng. I return to my. own small mite of material contributed to this discussion, and I take up the point which has been made against me, to the effect that, in this correspondence, I have ignored (or, more correctly, avoided) the great fundamental therms upon which Free Trade is built. These theories are well-known as the theories of exchange, and I have been tolerably familiar with them for over 20 years past. Mr. A. H. Crosfield and Mr. Charles r, . Parker, have made considerable use of these elementary theories of exchange, in their able letters; and very interesting it ia to read these clever disquisitions. Personally, how* ever, I prefer practice to theory; and, hence, I have carefully avoided confusing my judgment with the heaps of theoretical jargon, which I find in most of our standard words on political economy. Before Mr. Crosfleld, Mr. Parker, and the other clever advocates of Free Trade could convert me to their theories, they would have to prove that political economy is a science. By science, I mean a set of fixed, immutable, uni- versally-admitted laws, bearing upon any given subject The late Lord Justicelßyles, in his book "Sophisms of Fret Trade," written bylhim in 1849, said" If by a science be meant a collection of truths ascertained by experiment, and on whicliall well-informed men are agreed, then political economy is manifestly not yet a science. The political economy of Munn and Gee in 1750, was very different from the political economy of M'Cullock and Mill in 1850. But, perhaps it was not more different than the political economy of M'Cullock and Mill now is,from what will be the political economy of 1950. If by a science be meant a subject on which some little has gradually become known, but the great body of solid knowledge yet remains to be discovered by experience, observation, and patient thought, then indeed,in this lower sense,political economy is a science." what a mastery of our language, Byles had! Let us follow him a bit further, and we shall learn something apropos to the fiscal problems of 1903, though written in 1819 :—" Bat, if political economy claims to be a science at all, she must abate much of her pretensions, much of her dogmatism, descend to a lower rank, and adopt a more modest and inquiring tone; she must learn to tolerate doubt, to endure contradiction. If she aspires to learn in'the book of experience, she must ex- pect—as she turns over the leaves—to meet with problems unexpected,and ultimate solutions at variance with all preconceived notions ; she must make up her mind to see theory after theory—supported by great names and confidently propounded—yet, after all,reb Liked and exposed by experiment; she must remember that there are twenty wrong courses of public policy to one right one, and that all the erroneous ones are often tried before the right one can be demonstrated, by experience, to be right. A slow, prinful, hnmiliating road to knowledge, but the only right one. Other paths may lead to conjecture or opinion more or less plausible; this alone to certain ,and demonstrable knowledge. But what we want is not to conjecture, but to know. What experimental science is there in which the whole truth WiS discovered at once or in the course of a few years? Much less are we to suppose that we have been favoured with sudden and preternatural illumination on a subject so complex and difficult as political economy." How different is this modest language of the late Lord Justice Byles, from the "there-you-are-don't-you-know" cocksure theories and dogruatisms of the average free-trader of to-day. Just one more quotation from the Judge, and then I must pass on :—"There were many writers before Adam Smith, of whom posterity will form an estimate more lavourable than is now entertained. 'Bacon, Montes- quieu. F4nelon, Petty, Swift, and Voltaire will not, hereafter, be less esteemed because they did not use the s parade of scientific terms, and were not embarassed by modern and doubtful theories." It is a' matter for regret that the great book by Lord Justice Byles, is long since out of print. The fact of it having been writtfm in 1849, at the birth—as it were—of Free Trade, makes the book now all the more valuable and interesting, as we have fifty years of actual experience of free-trade, by which to measure the views he advanced. I had sketched various illustrations of religious, political, and physiological theories, long held as ortho- dox, and afterwards abandoned as untenable, but space forbids. With British trade in the sad condition it now is, I hope that my Free-Trade friends will trust lees to complex theoretical problems as to what Free Trade ought to do, and coolly and impar- tially test for themselves what it really is doing. One of the pet theories of Free Traders, is, that the cost of living is much higher in protected countries. Let me quote Mr. Carnegie's book "The Empire of Business," page 235 :—" It has been common in Britain to attribute the supposed higher cost of living, in the United States, to the effect of the tariff. Now a little consideration will show that this impression is not well-founded. The principal highly-taxeddirticlesamder the McKinley Bill, are five :—First, the extra-fine silks of France ;second,the fine woollens and linens ofßritain; third,the extra-fine linens of Germany'and France fourth, the high-priced wines of France; and fifth, Havana tobacco and cigars. The duties on all these are very high, woollens 60 per cent of their value, silk even higher; champagnes, 325. per dozen, etc., etc. This is our " Democratic " Budget. There is not a working man in America who uses any of these articles. It is considered good policy, thus to tax heavily the luxuries of the rich, and admit free the tea and coffee and raw sugar used by the masses. It is not probable that this policy will he reversed, in the United States, or even greatly modified, however much talk there may be of tariff reform." Passing along, Mr. Carnegie says that the American workman has much higher wages than the Briton; in fact, that the Briton—in the principal handicrafts, gets little more than one half the wages of an American; for those who wish exact figures, the proportion is fifty six and a half per cent. Mr. Carnegie makes the proposition very clear when he says, that, " Having higher revenues, the American is not content to live without what would be considered luxuries in any of the old countries of Europe. He earns more and he spends more. Therefore, in one sense it ie true that cost (If living,as the American lives, is greater than that of the Briton, at the standard that the Briton has to live. But it is none the less true that this arises from the fact that the two workmen live in a different manner. For those similar things which are absolutely necessary, the cost is much less in the newer land. The American workman and his family can live verycheaply indeed, if so inclined." On page 303, Mr. Carnegie reproduces an article he wrote, in 1898, for The Iron Age. I will just give a few significant lines, for our free-trade friends to reflect upon :—" Britain, and other foreign nations, have become our dumping ground for surplus iron and steel, which means 'more than the uninitiated would suspect. The Carnegie

WARRINGTON AND THE LIFEBOAT INSTITUTION.

Particulars of Next Sat urday Demonstration.

A WORTHY EFFORT.

,- For the third time in its history, Warrington will have a" Lifeboat Saturday" on September sth, and. efforts are being made to eclipse all previous efforts by holding a grand Fancy Dress Parade and Carnival. The Lifeboat Saturday movement was established in Warrington on the sth November, 1892, by the then Mayoress (Mrs, Edward Greenall). No. contribution has been made by the public of Warrington to the Royal National Lifeboat Institution since 1897 in con- sequence of the many calls which have been made upon the public in recent years for the South African war and local charities*. It was thought that the time had now arrived when a strong effort should be made to show in a practical form, the continued in- terest of Warrington people in an institution which has done such noble work.

The Mayor and Mayoyess'readily identified themselves with the movement, and at a meeting called by the Mayor in the early part of the year a strong and in- fluential committee was formed to make arrangements for a demonstration which will take place on Saturday. next, September sth. The various sub-committees have been busily engaged during the past few weeks in perfecting their arrangements and elaborate pre- parations have been made for making the affair as great a success as possible. It is hardly necessary to refer to the beneficent work of the Lifeboat Institution, but briefly it may be stated that the institution has the splendid record of having granted rewards since it was established in 1824 for the rescue of 44,000 lives. In an appeal - for help from all classes the local committee point out that the whole of the money raised by Lifeboat Saturday demonstrations, collections,- tcc., is devoted entirely to the payment of lifeboatmen, grants to their widows and children, Scc. Collecting boxes will be placed in the streets on Lifeboat Saturday as usual, and during the week boxes will be left at the hotels, clubs, etc. Anyone wishing to take charge of a box, or to promote collections should communicate with any member of the committee or Mr. Stanley Wilson (hon.'secretary) Town Hall. THE ORDER OF PROCESSION. '

Those taking part in the fancy dress parade and carnival will meet at the Recreation Ground, Town Hall at 4.30. Sergeant-Major Rawlinson will be the chief marshal of the procession. There will be nine sections and a marshal will be appointed in charge of each. Prizes to the value of about £2O will be offered in the following classes :—Neatest dressed lady, neatest dressed gentleman, best decorated machine, most original costume, most comical costume. The procession will start from the recreation ground at five o'clock prompt and after traversing theprincipal streets of the town will disperse at the Drill Hall where a grand concert will be held. The positions in the pro- cession have been drawn for by ballot and they will be as under. A motor car will announce the arrival of the procession at various points. SECTION No. E.—Detachment of Police, Ist Volunteer Battalion South Lancashire Regiment Band, Volunteer Mounted lafantry Section, Volunteer Cycle Corps.!! JosephCrosfield and Son's Exhibit, Minia- ture Ship on Limy (decorated), Warrington Cycling Club, the Lifeboat,,Busbie" and crew. FEATURES OF THE PROCESSTON.

It will be seenthat the procession will be of a varied character and possess many features of interest. The lifeboat" Ilusbie," which will form the roar of the pro- cession, will be manned by a full crew. The " Busbie " has been stationed at Trvine, Ayrshire. N. 8., since 1887. It has been instrumental in saving 33 lives. There will also be five fire engines in the procession, equipped with brigades, and a large number of comic characters, Morris dances and others. About 300 collec- tors'have been enrolled by the Collectors' Committee to Undertake the work of gathering in the poople's gifts. The members of the ladies' auxiliary under the presi- dency of the Mayoress, have for the past fortnight been canvassing the town and district, and have met with liberal support. It is hoped that the response of the public will enable the committee to forward at least £250 to the Lifeboat Institution. _ _ _

Briggs, M.A., Of St. Barnabas' Mission Church Chester, who has this week notified the patron (Rev, R. Greenall, Rector of Grappenhall),of his willingness to accept it. We understand that Mr. Briggs has had charge of the mission since 1897, and it is iateresting to note that previous to his acceptance of the living of. Christ church, the Rev. F. A. Screeton also worked in ttha parish No appointment has yet been made to the living of St. James's, Latchford.

Mr. MATHER: ,ft is only lighting and cleansing the old hospital to make it habitable for the additional inmates.

The CLERK said he would connuunicate with the Inspector. This was all the business of public interest.

MR. A. H. CROSFIELD AND THE WORKS BOWLING COMPETITION.

WORKS BOWLING ASSOCIATION HANDICAP. Under theauspices of the Warrington Works Bowling Association, a handicap was commenced on Saturd.Br onthe King and Queen green. No fewerthan 300 entries, have been received.

LIBERAL CLUB.

The annual bowling main for prizes presented by the President (Mr. A. 11.Crosfield) took place on the green of the club on Saturday in splendid weather. Therewas a capital entry, smd, as was expected, exceptional interest was shown in the games which were very keenly con- tested. The prizes will be presented at a "smoke, to take place on the return of the president from Switzerland. Se.veral local artistes have promised their The conclusion of the handicap took place on Tuesday evening, when the winners were as follows :-1, F. Jewkes ; 2, J. Parr ; 3, W. Daintith ; 4, J. Berry; 5, A. Mynekyme • 6, A, LPwlor ; 7, W. Patrick; 8, W. Dein- tith. This handicap brings the season to a close which has been most succesful. and the Captain (Mr. J. E. Warren) is to be praised for the way in which he has handledhis match teams. We learn that next seasolz there is every probability of the Club promoting a League to be confined to clubs in the town. AnywaY the suggestion is a good one, and further developments are awaited.

THE CROSFIELD CUP. "PRINCE OF WALES" LODGE OF ODDFELLOWS. Considerable interest centred in the first annual handicap in connection with the above lodge which took place on Saturday on the green of the Brooklands Hotel, Lovely-lane, in the presence of over 250 specta- tors. In addition to 16 prizes, there wasalso a beautiful silver cup presented for annual competition by Mr. A. H. Crosfield. The winner receives a handsome gold medal, on which is a representation of a bowler. For- tunately, the weather was very favourable. 'there were 64 entries, and at two o'clock the struggle was commenced. James Wood, T. Bolton, T. Reddish, J. T. Boardman, W. Haigh, R. Holland,and W. Kerfoot,are amongst the best players Warrington can boast of. W. Jones (the winner of the cup) disposed of T. Bolton (county player) by 13-10, and W. Haigh was beaten 131 F. Wisedale, 13-7. The Secretary (Mr. G. F. Freeman) with R. Holland defeated his luau 13.12 although Holland peeled at one time 12.5. 'nth& second round matters became much more lively, and stiffer games were noticed. F. Wisedale beat W. Kerfoot 13.11, T. Rigby beat T. Reddish 13.11. and 3. Wilkinson won from G. Bottomley 13.11. The rtamber of spectators had now increased, and owing to the excellent bowling cheers were numerous. The third round ended with Freeman vrowell 13.7, Atkinson v. J. Willett 13.11 ;Nv• Jpnes v &Took 13.9. There were eight left in the flourth round and, of course,the excitement was what can be readily imagined. Other results were; F. Wisedale E. Haigh 13.10. Atkinson v A. Cooper 13.3. J. Vf.• Elliott v. G. F. Freeman 13-11, and W. Jones v. d• Wrench 13-8. The fifth round ended with Atkinson defeating J. W. Elliot 13-11, and W. Jones defeatinl F. Wisedale 13-6. The rain had kept off until the final round had been reached, but the game was concluded bY W. Jones defeating Atkinson 13-5 amidst cheers, and of course he became the first proud possessor of the ouP and modal. The Provincial Grand Master (Mr. Ellis Clare) supported by D.G.M. Holford, T. Wadsworth (secretary of the lodge), and Farank Webster (corres- ponding secretary of the district) adjourned to the large room and they were well received. The GRAND MASTER complimented the competitors on the excellent way the handicap had been carried out, and remarked upon the generosity of Mr. A. H. Cros- field in giving a cup and medal—which would be pre- sented in due course. He also alluded to the fact that the competition brought them into contact with mem- bers who had practically never been seen since their_ initiation. He proceeded to:pay a tribute to the work of • the secretary and committee. The Deputy Grand Master and Messrs. T. Wadsworth and J. H. Norbury also addressed the gathering. The cup winner and runner-up said a few words whidi were loudly cheered.

The SECRETARY (Mr. G. F. Freeman) said that wtot honour had been given him he must extend to his cora' mittee and his chairman (Mr. T. P. Bradshaw), with tile best thanks to the host (Mr. J. Earle). The committee was composed as follows :—IVIessTS' W. King J. Massey, W. Cottom, H. Sykes, W. Whith9ls4 T. Reddish, H. Maddock (treasurer), G. F. Freenzals (secretary), andT. P. Bradshaw (chairman). The referee was Mr. T. Reddish, and the green stewards were Messrs' W. Whitharn, W. King, and H. Sykes.

Baptist Church, Golborne-street.

"John the Baptist" was the subject taken by thP pastor for his evening sermon on Sunday last, and was pointed out that although John's teaching seelns to have been too harsh, his denunciations too severe, and his gospel defective, yet he was a glorious charac- ter, and his gospel spread over the whole of the land' Many helpful and practical lessons were suggested by' the further study of his character under the three head' ings, What Christ thought of John, What he thought of himself, and What the people thought of him. His life of hardship and cruel death were touched upon, and the' subject of immortality briefly brought forward in con- clusion by the question, "Where is justice if the grave ends all ?"

STREET BETTING ATEA.RLESTOWN.—At Newton Peal Sessions on Thursday a number of cases of streel betting were heard. James Lees, Walter Waterman, au., Matthew Seton were each fined in the maximum penaltY of £5 and costs and similar fines were imposed on °tbe'. defendants for aiding and abetting.—Mr. A. Bro./0 conducted the prosecutions on behalf of the police.

The Committee does not bind itself to accept the lowest or any tender and reserves the right to divide any tender as may be deemed desirable. An assurance in writing will be required from the Con- tractor that he pays to the workmen employed by him the rate of wages recognised in the district and that he observes such hours of labour as are generally accepted as fair in each trade, producing whenever so required such evidence as may be deemed sufficient by the Corn mittee that he is paving such wages and observing such hours, Forms of tender which must, be reterned not later than the 12th September next, may be obtained from J. OUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON.- k ) EDUCATION COMMITTEE—SIICONDARY SCHOOL. THE IiDUCATION COMMITTEE INTEND OPENING A SECONDARY SCHOOL for boys and girls in the Technical Institute, Palmyra-square. The curriculum will extend over three or four years, and will meet the demand for a wider course ot education than is usually given in the Elementary Schools. The fee will be 9d per week 10s 6.1 per term, or 30s per annum, payable in advance. Intending scholars must be 12 years of age before August let. 1903. Owing to the limited accommodation an Entrance Examination will be held on the 11th and 12th September. Forms of application for admission may be obtained from J. MOORE MUhRAY, Secretary and Director. Education Offices, Warrington.
